                                      CHAPTER 1


                                                           Introduction



1.1   STUDY OBJECTIVES

This report contains details of the sedimentology, petrography, sequence stratigraphy and reservoir
properties of well South Pars-9 (SP-9), offshore Iran (Figure 1).  It was produced at the request of
Petropars and Enterprise. Details of the database provided are given in Section 1.2.

The core description took place at two scales: 1:25 for reservoir dominated sections, and 1:50 for
non-reservoir sections, as determined in discussion with Enterprise.  The depth intervals for the
different description scales are documented in Table 1.

The aims and objectives of this study were to:

•   provide a detailed core description at 1:25 and 1:50 scale;
•    integrate wireline data from those wells with wireline data with the detailed core description;
•    identify sequence boundaries within SP-9;
•    construct  facies,  diagenetic  and  sequence  stratigraphic  models  to  provide  a  detailed
     understanding of the reservoir characteristics and distribution at the SP-9, offshore Iran.

The database for the study is summarised in Table 2. A listing of sample depths for standard quick-
look and detailed thin section petrography is given in Tables 3a and 3b. The core depths for SP-9
were correlated with the wireline log responses by Enterprise Oil, UK, and provided to Robertson
Research International as the core to log shifts (Table 4).

1.2   DATABASE

Petropars/Enterprise supplied the following materials in order to meet the above aims and objectives
of this study:

•   core from SP-9;
•    wireline log suite for SP-9;
•   plug but-ends for thin section preparation;
•   conventional core analyses for SP-9;
•   core to log shifts;
•   core photographs.

The database provided by Enterprise and thin sections produced by Robertson are summarised in
Table 2.

1.3   REPORT FORMAT

This sedimentological study contains a  suite  of  text,  tables,  figures,  plates, appendices and
enclosures.

The text includes detailed discussion of the lithologies, lithofacies and depositional history, sequence
stratigraphy and the diagenetic controls on porosity-permeability. The integration of the lithofacies
and diagenetic effects is discussed in Chapter 6, where the reservoir facies and reservoir quality are
investigated. All the main results of the study are summarised in the Executive Summary (Chapter 2)
and a reference section is included.

The appendices  consist of the  results of the  brief and  detailed petrographic  analysis, study
procedures, glossary of selected geological terms and the Robertson Standard Legend of symbols
used on the enclosures (Volume 2).

The SP-9 well is illustrated by eight data summary charts (Enclosures 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7 at 1:25 scale
and Enclosures 1, 4 and 8 at 1:50 scale). The data summary charts display data collected from the
core  description and  petrography, along with summary comments,  interval  descriptions and
interpretations.

1.4   STUDY APPROACH

1.4.1   Analytical Phase

The core description and sampling phase involved the detailed description of 445m of core from SP-
9 at 1:25 and 1:50 scale (Table 1). From the core description, data were obtained on sedimentary
texture and structures including lithology, grain size, grain and fossil types, Dunham classification
(1962), burrow structures, diagenetic effects, oil stain and pore types visible to the naked eye. The
detailed core description provides a solid core database for the sequence stratigraphic and facies
association interpretation of the well. During core description the samples for petrographic analysis
were picked to reflect the main core lithofacies and to investigate unusual intervals.

The thin sections were prepared from plugs and rock chips taken from the core and used for
petrographic analysis. The petrographic analyses are split, with 200 samples having been examined
in detail, and a further 210 samples examined briefly to calibrate Dunham texture, or to assess grains
or diagenesis.  Parameters recorded during detailed petrographic analysis include details of the
microfabric, microtexture, microfacies, grain types, diagenetic effects and products, and pore types.

1.4.2   Integration and Interpretation Phase

The integration and interpretation phase of the study was used to combine all the data gathered from
the analysis phase with data provided by Petropars and Enterprise and in-house Robertson knowledge
of the reservoirs of the Middle East, in order to formulate depositional and diagenetic models and to
assess variations in reservoir quality within the well.

                                      CHAPTER 2


                                                  Executive Summary



The aims of this study were to construct facies, diagenetic and sequence stratigraphic models of the
Kangan and Dalan reservoirs to provide a detailed understanding of the petrophysical characteristics
and distribution at the SP-9 well, offshore Iran. This involved detailed core description, petrography
and integration of other data such as wireline logs.

Deposition occurred on an extensive carbonate platform in arid conditions during the Late Permian -
Early Triassic. Cyclicity on different scales is apparent and the succession consists of a series of
stacked shallowing upwards cycles in the order of 2-10m thick.  These small-scale cycles form
packages termed depositional units and are probably fourth order cycles. ?Third order cycles termed
depositional sequences were recognised corresponding to KS4a, KS4b, KS3a, KS3b, KS2, KS1a and
KS1b. The largest scale of cyclicity were four major sequences, the KS1 to KS4.

The typical sequence composing the depositional units is a middle shelf facies association deposited
below fair weather wave base shallowing to a tidal flat environment often with the development of
evaporitic pools and the precipitation of anhydrite. Diagenetic anhydrite may also be associated with
these supratidal sabkha deposits.

The transgressive systems tract of the depositional units often comprises mudstones of the middle
shelf facies association suggesting a rapid deepening. The highstand systems tract is typified by the
deposition of grainstones of the barrier shoal complex as shallow water conditions favourable for
ooid formation came into existence. Lagoons developed behind the barrier shoal complex with the
degree to which water circulation was restricted being a function of the effectiveness of the shoal
complex to behave as a barrier. Discrete grainstone beds are interbedded with fine grained lagoonal
sediments and represent reworking of shoal complex sediment into the lagoon. The  tidal  flat
environment is represented by a variety of depositional facies reflecting subtle changes in base level
and accommodation space. Late highstand supratidal conditions signify the most extreme shallowing
and are represented by anhydrite either precipitated from saline pools or sabkhas. Often depositional
units are capped by laminar sediments which overlie anhydrite. This suggests the anhydrite formed
within the tidal flat or the base level turnaround point had been reached and the transgressive systems
tract of the next depositional unit had started.

KS4 comprises a large proportion of shoal grainstones, whilst KS3 is dominated by well developed
mudstone, wackestone and boundstone tidal flat sediments. KS2 indicates a return to the barrier
shoal complex facies association. KS1 has an increase in the proportion of late highstand anhydrite,
tidal flat and lagoonal sediments together with a good proportion of shoal grainstones. Overall the
sequence appears comparable with adjacent wells.

The major diagenetic processes and products that have affected the depositional porosity in these
wells include carbonate dissolution (leaching), cementation, anhydrite formation, dolomitisation,
                                    Page 3 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 12 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


compaction and pressure solution, and the development of microporosity. A variety of fractures
occur but the most common are microfractures. Fracturing may not significantly enhance the
properties of the reservoir.

Reservoir Facies Summary

In order to assess reservoir quality of the KS1 to KS4 reservoir facies were identified and defined as
petrophysically similar rock units which are a function of their original depositional lithofacies and
later diagenetic overprint. Fifteen reservoir facies were recognised grading from RF1 having the best
reservoir quality to RF15 with the poorest.

RF1 (Porosity: >22%, Permeability: >150 mD) occurs predominantly as dolomitised sediments of the
barrier shoal complex facies association with relict intergranular and intercrystalline porosity and
excellent connectivity.

RF2 (Porosity: 27%-35%, Permeability: 10-200 mD) has good reservoir quality and is dominated by
extremely finely crystalline dolomitised mudstones of the foreshoal, burrowed shoal and restricted
lagoon  facies  associations with extremely  fine  intercrystalline macroporosity and very good
connectivity.

RF3 (Porosity: 12%-21%, Permeability: 100-2200 mD) has very good reservoir quality and  is
composed of dolomitised ooid and  bioclastic grainstones of the barrier shoal complex facies
association with relict intergranular porosity. Connectivity is excellent via intercrystalline and relict
intergranular pore throats.

RF4 (Porosity: 13%-27%, Permeability: 10-200 mD) has moderate to good reservoir quality, is
generally composed of dolomitised fine to medium grained peloidal and oolitic packstones and
grainstones or very finely crystalline planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite, representing the barrier shoal
complex and lagoon facies associations, respectively. Relict intergranular porosity is interconnected
via intercrystalline microporosity (<20 µm pore size).

RF5 (Porosity: 16%-27%, Permeability: 1-100 mD) has moderate to good reservoir quality and
consists of oolitic, calcite cemented grainstones and fine grained dolomitised peloidal grainstones of
the barrier shoal complex and foreshoal washovers. Extensive leaching of ooids produced abundant
mouldic pores isolated by intergranular calcite cements with poor connectivity.

RF6 (Porosity: 24%-47%, Permeability: 0.07-7.3 mD) has moderate reservoir quality, composed of
fine to very fine grained calcite cemented, oolitic grainstones. As with RF5, extensive leaching of
ooids produced abundant mouldic pores  isolated by  intergranular  calcite cements with poor
connectivity.

RF7 (Porosity: 1.5%-19%, Permeability: 0.01-30 mD) has moderate to poor reservoir  quality,
composed of dolomitised mudstones with moderate intercrystalline microporosity, very fine grained
dolomitised  peloidal packstones with  intercrystalline microporosity and ooid grainstones with
varying amounts of mouldic porosity.

RF8 (Porosity: 7%-13%, Permeability: 10-600 mD) has moderate reservoir quality, is dominated by
medium grained, dolomitised ooid grainstones of the barrier shoal complex facies association, with
low to moderate amounts of anhydrite cementation in relict intergranular pores and moderate to poor
connectivity.

                                    Page 4 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 13 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


RF9 (Porosity: 15%-22%, Permeability: 0.05-0.5 mD) has poor reservoir quality and is composed of
medium to fine grained oolitic grainstones with cemented intergranular pores and mouldic pores
partially occluded by planar-e dolomite reducing connectivity.

RF10 (Porosity: 3%-11%, Permeability: 0.8-70 mD) has poor reservoir quality, consists of medium
grained, partially anhydrite cemented, dolomitised ooid and peloidal grainstones of the barrier shoal
complex and foreshoal washovers facies associations.

RF11  (Porosity: 3%-10%, Permeability: 50-2000 mD) has moderate  to good reservoir quality
because of the high permeability associated with micro-fractures in these dolomitised sediments.
Petrophysical character is controlled by diagenesis not facies.

RF12 (Porosity: 7%-13%, Permeability: 0.05-3.5 mD) has very poor reservoir quality dominated by
tight, calcite cemented ooid and peloidal grainstones of the barrier shoal complex facies association.
Primary intergranular and secondary mouldic porosity has been greatly reduced.

RF13 (Porosity: 0.01%-8.5%, Permeability: 0.01-10 mD) has very poor reservoir quality with
extensively anhydrite cemented and replaced grainstone and packstone sediments from a variety of
facies. Petrophysical character is controlled by diagenesis not facies.

RF14 (Porosity: 0.2%-4%, Permeability: 6-150 mD) has poor reservoir quality and permeability is
the  result  of  microfracturing  of  the  predominantly  mudstone  and  wackestone  lithofacies.
Petrophysical character is controlled by diagenesis not facies.

RF15  (Porosity: 0.01%-9%,  Permeability:  0.01-2 mD)  has  extremely poor  reservoir  quality,
comprises a  large proportion  of  oolitic and  peloidal  grainstones of  the  barrier shoal  facies
association, that are extensively cemented by calcite or dolomite. Mudstones and wackestones from
foreshoal, lagoon and  tidal  flat facies associations are commonly partially dolomitised with a
completely neomorphically recrystallised calcite matrix; consequently, any microporosity or mouldic
porosity has all but been destroyed.

Sequence Stratigraphic Summary

KS4a depositional sequence is dominated by poor quality reservoir facies, predominantly RF10,
RF11, RF13, RF14 and RF15.

KS4b depositional sequence is dominated by good to excellent reservoir facies (RF1-7), with only
minor units of poor reservoir quality KS4b-1 and 2.  Thin poor reservoir quality streaks occur
throughout and are generally correlatable with foreshoal and tidal flat mudstones.

KS3a depositional sequence is dominated by poor quality reservoir facies (RF13, RF15) which,
where locally fractured, are of slightly better quality (RF14). Within these tight sediments thin
laminae of relatively good quality reservoir facies (RF2, RF5 and RF7) occur at the base of the
KS3a-2 and -3 depositional units.

KS3b depositional sequence is dominated by relatively poor quality reservoir facies (RF13, 15, 10
and 11)  reflecting  the abundance  of poorer  reservoir  quality,  matrix-rich  facies  associations
(foreshoal, lagoon and intertidal flats) and the significant amount of anhydrite cementation.

KS2 major sequence is dominated by barrier shoal sediments (BS) with a wide range of reservoir
quality. The best reservoir quality is developed in KS2-2 and comprises calcite and dolomitised

oolitic and relict grain grainstones and dolomitised mudstones and wackestones. The remainder of
the KS2 sequence comprises poor quality reservoir facies.

KS1 major sequence has predominantly low to no reservoir quality (RF13 and RF15) with local units
of good quality reservoir present (predominantly RF4, RF6 and RF8) as thin units of calcitic and
dolomitised oolitic grainstones separated by thick non-reservoir units.

                                                    Reservoir Geological Study of Cores from the
                            SP-9 Well (Kangan and Dalan Formations), South Pars Field, Iran


                                      CHAPTER 3


                                    Lithofacies and Sequence Stratigraphy



3.1   INTRODUCTION

In this chapter, the lithofacies identified and interpreted from detailed core description, detailed and
quick-look petrographic analysis of thin sections are discussed. The ultimate aim of these studies is
to predict and interpret reservoir quality characteristics within well SP-9 and to attempt to predict
lateral continuity and lateral variation of the constituent sediments of reservoir intervals and cycles
identified in the Kangan and Dalan Formations.

On the basis of data obtained from detailed core description and petrography, the cored intervals
were subdivided into lithofacies based on the texture of the original sediment (Dunham, 1962).

Eight lithofacies have been recognised (Table 5), they are:

1.   Oolitic grainstones and packstones
2.   Peloidal grainstones and packstones
3.   Bioclastic grainstones and packstones
4.   Intraclastic grainstones and packstones
5.   Relict grain grainstones and packstones
6.  Mudstones and wackestones
7.   Algal packstones, mudstones and boundstones
8.   Anhydrite

Within each lithofacies a number of sub-lithofacies were identified based on variations in lithology,
component fossil assemblages and grains and sedimentary structures. In addition, the sediments
encountered in SP-9 have a strong diagenetic overprint that has to be taken into consideration within
the lithofacies scheme. A large proportion of the sediments have been dolomitised (58.6%) with
varying degrees of retention of depositional fabric. Dolomitised equivalent sub-lithofacies have also
been included in the depositional facies where  it is possible to identify the original depositional
texture and grains. The terminology of the various fabrics seen within the dolomite sub-lithofacies
follows Sibley and Gregg (1987). In  total, 28 sub-lithofacies have been identified to include
diagenetic overprint.

The relative composition of the well, by lithofacies and lithology, is summarised in Table 6.

Descriptions of the lithofacies are given below and are also included on the data summary charts
(Enclosures 1 to 8), using the coding system (Table 5). The descriptions below include a summary of
the main features seen in each lithofacies and their constituent sub-lithofacies.

                                    Page 7 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 16 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


The distribution of lithofacies within the cored wells, in the context of the sequence stratigraphic
framework, is shown on Enclosures 1-8. The lithofacies identified from the core provide the basis
for defining and interpreting the facies associations and depositional environments of the sequence
(see Section 3.3).

3.2   LITHOFACIES TYPES

3.2.1   Oolitic Grainstones and Packstones

Oolitic grainstones and packstones comprise 29.6% of the SP-9 well and contribute some of the best
reservoir facies (Plate 2.1).

3.2.1.1 Oolitic lime grainstone and packstone (OGl)

The oolitic lime grainstone and packstone sub-lithofacies is characterised by ooids/coated grains as
the predominant component grain types. The ooid grainstones and packstones rarely to commonly
contain  other  grain  types,  including  incipient  ooids with poorly developed  oolitic  coatings,
intraclasts, peloids, bivalves, gastropods, green algae, benthonic foraminifera and indeterminate
skeletal debris. The modal grain size varies from very fine lower to medium upper. Grainstone
depositional textures predominate, together with a high proportion of mud lean packstones and rare
packstones.  The  oolitic intervals are predominantly massive; however, sedimentary structures
include planar  horizontal and cross-lamination with  rare trough  cross-lamination. The  oolitic
intervals are generally present exhibiting fining upwards sequences from coarse to medium or
medium to fine grained. However, locally, coarsening upwards sequences were also rarely observed.
The OGl units were observed in varying thicknesses, from very thinly interbedded units, with or
without basal lags, to extremely thick stacked series of oolitic grainstones which generally fined up
within each unit and overall throughout the interval.

Within this well the oolitic grains have commonly been leached, leaving abundant mouldic pores
(Plates 3.5 and 3.6). These mouldic pores are often rimmed by early marine cements which has the
effect  of  isolating  the pores and decreasing  connectivity. This  is  reflected  in  the  porosity-
permeability characteristics of the lithofacies (e.g. Figures 3 and 11).

3.2.1.2 Dolomitised oolitic grainstone and packstone (OGd)

The dolomitised oolitic grainstone and packstone (OGd) sub-lithofacies is typically depositionally
similar to that of the OGl, with similar sedimentary structures and accessory grains; however, it has
undergone extensive dolomitisation.   In the OGd sub-lithofacies the  oolitic grains have been
dolomitised (Plate 2.1), commonly to the point where only vague ghosts of the grains are visible
under thin section. Petrographic analysis indicates that the dolomite fabric ranges from planar-e
(idiotopic) to non-planar (xenotopic).

The dolomitisation of the grains and lack of intergranular cement results in the dominance of relict
intergranular and intercrystalline porosity.

3.2.1.3 Oolitic intraclastic lime grainstone and packstone (OIGl)

The oolitic intraclastic lime grainstones and packstones (OIGl) are depositionally similar to the OGl
sub-lithofacies, but they are dominated by limestone intraclasts as well as ooids, resulting in a very
coarse to pebble grain size in core.  The intraclasts are generally locally derived, subrounded

                                    Page 8 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 17 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


limestones. The OIGl sediments are commonly present at the bases of ooid units with a sharp contact
which may be marked by a stylolite, solution seam, burrowed surface or obvious grain size change.

Reservoir quality relationships are generally similar to those of the OGl sub-lithofacies and as such,
mouldic porosity from the leaching of ooids is dominant.

3.2.1.4 Dolomitised oolitic intraclastic grainstone and packstone (OIGd)

The dolomitised oolitic intraclastic grainstone and packstone sub-lithofacies is depositionally and
texturally similar to the OIGl sub-lithofacies, but has been subsequently dolomitised. Generally, the
intraclasts are limestones or dolomitised limestones and the same basal boundary relationships are
seen as in the OIGl sub-lithofacies. Petrographic analysis indicates that the dolomite fabric ranges
from planar-e (idiotopic) to non-planar (xenotopic).

The reservoir quality relationship seen in the OIGd sub-lithofacies is similar to that of the OGd, with
intergranular and  intercrystalline  porosity dominating the  best  reservoir  quality  intervals and
anhydrite cementing the poorer quality intervals.

3.2.2   Peloidal Grainstones and Packstones

This lithofacies exhibits variable reservoir quality, from poor to very good and comprises 17.3% of
the total cored interval (Plate 2.2).

3.2.2.1 Peloidal lime grainstone and packstone (PGl)

The peloidal lime grainstone and packstone sub-lithofacies is characterised by micritic peloids and
micropeloids as the predominant component grain types (Plate 2.2). Other grain types commonly
present include incipient ooids with poorly developed oolitic coatings, intraclasts, pellets, bivalves,
green algae, gastropods, benthonic foraminifera and indeterminate skeletal debris. Grain size varies
from very fine lower to medium, however, the modal grain size is typically very fine to fine grained.
Mud lean packstone (<10% matrix) and packstone depositional textures predominate together with a
significant proportion of grainstones.  Sedimentary structures are very variable with common
massive, bioturbated, planar horizontal and cross-laminated units.  These units typically occur as
either thin, constant grain size intervals which may or may not have a basal lag, or as thicker,
medium to fine grained, fining upwards sequences

Intergranular pores dominate the macroporosity; however, microporosity (pores <20µ in size) is
commonly significant occurring within the matrix in intergranular areas of the packstones. Mouldic
pores may therefore be interconnected by microporosity.

3.2.2.2 Dolomitised peloidal grainstone and packstone (PGd)

The dolomitised peloidal grainstones and packstones are depositionally similar to the PGl sub-
lithofacies; however, both grains and matrix have been dolomitised (Plate 3.4). Petrographic analysis
indicates that the dolomite fabric ranges from planar-e (idiotopic) to non-planar (xenotopic).

The dolomitisation of the grains has generally resulted in relatively good intercrystalline porosity in
the fine grained PGd sediments.  The poor reservoir quality PGd intervals are typically anhydrite
cemented with permeability increasing with the reduction in anhydrite cement.

                                    Page 9 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 18 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


3.2.2.3 Peloidal intraclastic lime grainstone and packstone (IPGl)

The peloidal  intraclastic lime grainstones and packstones are relatively  rare.  The IPGl sub-
lithofacies is depositionally similar to the PGl sediments, but is dominated by subrounded limestone
intraclasts and peloids.

3.2.2.4 Dolomitised peloidal intraclastic grainstone and packstone (IPGd)

The dolomitised peloidal intraclastic grainstones and packstones (IPGd) are relatively rare.  The
IPGd sediments are depositionally similar to the PGd sediments, but are dominated by subrounded
limestone intraclasts and peloids.

Generally, the PGd sediments have been strongly anhydrite cemented and have poor porosity-
permeability characteristics.

3.2.3   Bioclastic Grainstones and Packstones

The bioclastic grainstones and packstones comprise 2.1 % of the sediments in SP-9 and generally
exhibit either relatively good or relatively poor reservoir quality (Plate 2.3).

3.2.3.1 Bioclastic lime grainstone and packstone (BGl)

The bioclastic lime grainstone and packstone sub-lithofacies is characterised by the presence of
abundant, varied bioclasts (Plate 2.3).  The dominant grain types include green algae, benthonic
foraminifera, bivalves and molluscs, with subordinate peloids, ooids, oncoids, ostracods, gastropods,
echinoderm fragments and indeterminate skeletal debris rarely to commonly present.  Grain size
varies from very fine lower to coarse lower, but is generally medium grained. Grainstone and mud
lean packstone depositional textures dominate with rarer packstones.  Sedimentary structures vary
from massive to planar lamination and low angle cross lamination defined by thin grainy laminae.

The BGl sub-lithofacies varies from having significant open mouldic porosity from the dissolution of
aragonitic components, to being variably calcite cemented with moderate porosity and local matrix
microporosity (pore  size <20µ)  in packstones. Connectivity  will therefore be via the matrix
micropore system and low permeability is likely.

3.2.3.2 Dolomitised bioclastic grainstone and packstone (BGd)

The dolomitised bioclastic grainstone and packstone sub-lithofacies is depositionally similar to the
BGl sub-lithofacies with generally the same range of sedimentary structures. The BGd sediments are
commonly anhydrite cemented resulting in poor porosity-permeability.

3.2.4   Intraclastic Grainstones and Packstones

The intraclastic grainstones and packstones comprise 0.9% of the sediments in SP-9. Due to the
bimodal nature of the grain size of this lithofacies it is typically only identified from core description.

3.2.4.1 Intraclastic lime grainstone and packstone (IGl)

The major component of this sub-lithofacies is subrounded, very coarse to pebble sized limestone
intraclasts. The intraclasts are mainly composed of micrite, although may be locally grainy and are
the result of rip-up of pre-existing sediments, which appear to have been lime mudstones although

                                    Page 10 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 19 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


this is difficult to confirm because of the small size of the intraclasts in core and the lack of adequate
petrographic control. Locally, the rip-up clasts exhibit algal lamination and are derived from a local,
underlying algal limestone lithofacies. Associated with intraclasts are peloids, occasional oolitically
coated grains, bivalve fragments and indeterminate skeletal debris. The IGl sediments are generally
massive, relatively thin (<0.5m), and often form the basal part of an overall fining upward sequence
of peloidal, oncolitic or oolitic limestones or dolomites.  The IGl intervals are thought to reflect
either the initial flood over the transgressive surface, or storm events when they are not generically
linked to the overlying sediments.

3.2.4.2 Dolomitised intraclastic grainstone and packstone (IGd)

The dolomitised  intraclastic  grainstone and packstone (IGd)  sub-lithofacies  is  depositionally,
texturally and structurally similar to the IGl sub-lithofacies.

3.2.5   Relict Grain Grainstones and Packstones

The relict grain grainstones and packstones are completely dolomite replaced and the original grain
cannot be identified. They comprise 3.8% of the sediments in SP-9 (Plate 2.4).

3.2.5.1 Dolomitised relict grain grainstone and packstone (RGd)

Dolomitised relict grain grainstone and packstone is characterised by completely dolomite replaced
grains, where the original grain cannot be identified either in core or thin section (Plate 2.4).
Typically, the RGd sediments are massive or vaguely bioturbated with rare grainy lamination.

The porosity-permeability characteristics of the RGd sediments are generally poor, reflecting the
pervasive dolomitisation (planar-e, idiotopic to non-planar, xenotopic fabrics) and probable anhydrite
cementation. However, locally, there are relatively permeable RGd sediments, exhibiting significant
intercrystalline macroporosity (Plate 2.4).

3.2.6  Mudstones and Wackestones

The mudstone and wackestone lithofacies (Plate 2.5) is one of the most common lithofacies in well
SP-9, comprising 22.6 % of the cored interval.

3.2.6.1 Lime mudstone and wackestone (MWl)

The  lime  mudstone  and  wackestone  (MWl)   sub-lithofacies   is  characterised  by  lime
mud/neomorphically recrystallised matrix supported sediments.  Petrographically, up to 26% of this
lithofacies is comprised of grains, with common to abundant replaced or relict grains also present
floating within the matrix.  The grains present include: peloids, bivalves, benthonic foraminifera,
indeterminate skeletal debris, green algae and sponge spicules. The structures within the MWl sub-
lithofacies are very variable reflecting the variable depositional environments. The MWl sediments
are typically massive to indistinctly laminated with solution seams and common bioturbation by
Zoophycos, Chondrites, Thallassinoides grain-filled burrows and indeterminate horizontal burrows.

The MWl  lithofacies  typically  has  a  relatively low  porosity and  is dominated by  matrix
microporosity (<20µ pore  size) with low permeability. The sediments may also be  partially
dolomitised further reducing available pore space.

                                    Page 11 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 20 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


3.2.6.2 Dolomitised mudstone and wackestone (MWd)

The dolomitised mudstone and wackestone (MWd) sub-lithofacies is characterised by a dolomitised
matrix supported sediment (Plates 2.5, 3.2 and 3.7).  Relict peloids are common in the majority of
samples, with accessory  relict benthonic  foraminifera,  bivalves, echinoderms,  ooids, oncoids,
brachiopods, green algae and gastropods rarely to commonly present.  The structures seen in the
MWd sub-lithofacies are similar to those seen in the MWl, with the addition of more strongly
developed planar lamination, grainy laminae, poorly developed algal lamination and algal structures,
rootlets and rare ruptured laminae/tepee structures.

The MWd sub-lithofacies shows a range of porosity-permeability characteristics from tight, clean,
poor  reservoir  quality  mudstones,  with  a  non-planar  (xenotopic)  dolomite  texture,  through
microcrystalline  dolomites  with  micro-intercrystalline  porosity  to  sediments  dominated  by
intercrystalline macroporosity, with a planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite texture.

3.2.7   Algal Packstones, Mudstones and Boundstones

The algal packstones, mudstones and boundstones together comprise 5.78% of the total cored
interval in SP-9, the majority of which is dolomitised (5.4%).

3.2.7.1 Dolomitised algal mat intraclast packstone (AMPd)

The dolomitised algal mat intraclast packstone (AMPd) sub-lithofacies consists of angular algal rip-
up clasts in an algal packstone/boundstone matrix. No core analysis data were available for this sub-
lithofacies; however, it is thought that it is likely to have a poor reservoir quality.

3.2.7.2 Dolomitised oncolitic grainstone and packstone (ONGd)

This lithofacies is characterised by large, pebble sized, oncoids (possible oncoid shown in Plate 2.6).
The oncoids are the result of algal coating of various type of nuclei of which gastropods and
benthonic foraminifera (miliolids) are the most important in this lithofacies. Other grain types
include ooids and oolitically coated grains, peloids, intraclasts, algal lumps and benthonic bivalves.
Sedimentary structures are generally absent with only rare, vague, discontinuous lamination because
of the generally coarse but variable grain size obscuring these features. This sub-lithofacies was only
seen in a dolomitised form, which is thought to reflect the rarity of the sub-lithofacies and its
occurrence within a pervasively dolomitised interval, rather than a diagenetic trend of this particular
sub-lithofacies.  No  macroporosity was  observed  within  the ONGd  sediments  during  core
description, and porosity never exceeded 1% in the core plugs from this sub-lithofacies, reflecting the
cementation of any remaining porosity by poikilotopic and blocky anhydrite. No oncolitic lime
grainstones and packstones were seen in this well.

3.2.7.3 Dolomitised rootlet/fenestral mudstone (RFMd)

This relatively minor sub-lithofacies is characterised, in core, by an abundance of dark fenestrae or
rootlets within a relatively clean mudstone (Plate 1.1). Other structures seen include ruptured algal
laminae, possible bored surfaces and long,  vertical, worm burrows.   Porosity-permeability  is
generally poor; however, within some reservoir layers (4, 9 and 10)  it  is enhanced by better
developed intercrystalline pore systems.

                                    Page 12 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 21 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


3.2.7.4 Thrombolitic lime boundstone (TBl)

Relatively minor, this sub-lithofacies is characterised by unlaminated stromatolite structures termed
‘thrombolites’. These structures have a ‘finger’-like morphology with dimensions of up to about 1 cm
in diametre and just a few centimetres long. They grew upwards from the substrate and are often
preserved in the core in growth position. The intervening sediments are usually composed of fairly
coarse grained grainstones and packstones.

3.2.7.5 Dolomitised thrombolitic boundstone (TBd)

The dolomitised thrombolitic boundstones (TBd) form the majority of the thrombolitic boundstones
seen in SP-9 (Plate  1.2).  The same structures  that characterise the TBl sub-lithofacies also
characterise the TBd, but the sediment is pervasively dolomitised. Porosity-permeability is generally
poor, but may be enhanced where intercrystalline porosity has been developed during dolomitisation.

3.2.7.6 Laminated lime stromatolitic boundstone (LSBl)

Laminated lime stromatolitic boundstones (LSBl) were only seen in Reservoir Layer 6 and were
dominated by undulose, cryptalgal lamination with local thin, grainy laminae and rare mottling.

3.2.7.7 Dolomitised laminated stromatolitic boundstone (LSBd)

A variety of structures of dolomitised algal stromatolites were seen in core, but have been grouped
together in this study.  The two end-members seen in core were stratiform, algally laminated
stromatolites, and more domal stromatolites.  The stratiform laminated sediments generally have
undulose, cryptalgal lamination with thin grainy laminae, with local fenestrae, burrows, intraclasts
and oncoids. The rare domal algal stromatolites are relatively large (over 0.5m high) and internally
laminated on a mm to cm scale. The LSBd intervals occur in beds up to 1m thick and are usually
associated with other algal sub-lithofacies such as the TBl/TBd, AMPd or RFMl/RFMd at the top of
shallowing cycles.

3.2.8   Anhydrite

Anhydrite is present in variable amounts throughout SP-9 especially towards the base of the cored
interval in the Nar Formation.  It totals 8.5% of the cored interval.  It is tight with no reservoir
potential and probably behaves as intra-reservoir seals or barriers to vertical flow.

3.2.8.1 Chickenwire mosaic anhydrite (AC)

The chickenwire mosaic anhydrite is observed as thin intervals throughout the SP-9 well, but is a
dominant lithology towards the base of the cored interval (Plate 1.2).  This sub-facies is typically
white to light grey in colour and comprises round and elongate anhydrite nodules, about 3 cm to 8 cm
in size which coalesce into large nodules in the chickenwire form. The individual nodules may be
tightly packed and distorted against each other indicating displacive growth or there may be variable
amounts of internodular host sediments of both limestone and dolomite.

3.2.8.2 Mottled anhydrite (AM)

The mottled anhydrite is one of the best developed forms of anhydrite and may be a sub-group of the
mosaic chickenwire and nodular anhydrite (Plate 1.3). The mottled anhydrite occurs where there has

                                    Page 13 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 22 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


been almost total replacement of the matrix by anhydrite, leaving only small wisps of the original
sediment.

3.2.8.3 Laminated and nodular anhydrite (AL)

This anhydrite is generally of darker appearance than chickenwire anhydrite, being grey to brownish
grey in colour with light grey interlaminations (Plate 1.5). The development of the laminations and
nodules is variable, from crudely layered to well layered and is commonly interbedded with more
nodular anhydrite. The crudely layered anhydrite generally has a massive internal structure with dark
argillaceous or organic rich contacts between each layer, or has a nodular appearance. Within the
finely layered anhydrite, the laminations are undulating and variable in thickness and occasionally
show evidence of  distortion due to localised displacive growth.  The laminated and nodular
anhydrites commonly have a sharp upper and/or lower boundary with the adjacent lithofacies, which
are commonly peloidal or oolitic grainstones.

3.2.8.4 Chevron and Palisade anhydrite (AP)

Chevron anhydrite is common towards the base of the well with a characteristic angular texture,
probably a result of the replacement of gypsum butterfly twins (Plate 1.4).  Palisade anhydrite is
locally present with strongly developed palisade crystals, commonly appearing to grow upwards and
outwards from a distinct surface, which may be recrystallised or locally compacted.  Palisade
anhydrite is generally associated with dark grey, organic rich laminae. The chevron and palisade
anhydrite is commonly interbedded or grades into nodular and laminated anhydrite.

3.2.8.5 Soft sediment disturbed/algal replacement anhydrite (AR)

The soft sediment disturbed/algal replacement anhydrite (Plates 1.6 and 1.7) is present at the base
SP-9, associated with the nodular and palisade anhydrite. The soft sediment disturbance is likely to
be the result of the growth of the anhydrite, but may also  reflect pre-existing soft sediment
deformation or cryptalgal lamination.

3.3   FACIES ASSOCIATIONS AND DEPOSITIONAL ENVIRONMENTS

The lithofacies identified from the cored intervals of SP-9 provide a basis from which facies
associations, the depositional model and, ultimately, the distribution and geometry of sedimentary
bodies  can  be  determined.  Although  lithofacies  provide  information  regarding  depositional
environments of specific sedimentary intervals, they do not provide an overall interpretation of the
deposition of the sedimentary sequence or give any insight into lateral or spatial variations of the
sediments, or sediment geometry. Facies associations were determined by grouping lithofacies that
were considered  to be  genetically and environmentally  related, each representing a  different
depositional setting. Larger scale features identified from the core, such as shallowing upwards
sequences, as well as smaller scale features recognised from core and during petrographic analysis
were considered in order to determine facies associations present in the wells.

Four facies associations have been identified: Foreshoal (FS), Barrier Shoal Complex (BS), Lagoon
(L) and Tidal Flat (TF). The characteristics of each facies association are described and interpreted
below and summarised in Table 7 and their top depths identified from the core are given in Table 8.
The facies associations comprise a number of component depositional facies, each of which is
incorporated  into  the  descriptions below.  The  depositional model based upon  these  facies
associations is schematically represented in Figure 2.

                                    Page 14 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 23 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


3.3.1   Foreshoal (FS)

The foreshoal facies association comprises open marine shelf sediments that were deposited in
relatively deep water, below fairweather wave base (although still within the photic zone) in a low
energy environment seaward of the barrier shoal facies association (Figure 2). The foreshoal facies
association comprises the sub-wavebase foreshoal (FSa) and the coarser grained shoal spillovers
(FSb) depositional facies.

The subtidal foreshoal (FSa) consists of MWl and MWd sub-lithofacies, with common bioturbation
and bioturbation mottling, vague lamination and open marine fauna including echinoderms and
sponge spicules. The grainy shoal spillovers (FSb) predominantly comprise the OGd, PGd and PGl
lithofacies, are massive, mottled,  oolitic or  peloidal, shoal derived  units which are  typically
interbedded or associated with the subtidal foreshoal. They were deposited during storm events as
sediment was derived from the adjacent barrier shoal complex.  The geometry of the subtidal
foreshoal (FSa) is likely to be laterally extensive, being deposited over a wide shelf area.  The
spillovers will have a more limited distribution and be confined to localised areas adjacent to the
barrier shoal complex.

3.3.2   Barrier Shoal Complex (BS)

The shoal complex is composed of four main depositional facies: the burrowed shoal shoreface
(BSd), shoal shoreface (BSc), shoal interior (BSb) and backshoal (BSa) each of which are described
below. The barrier shoal depositional facies have the best reservoir quality and geometrically, the
shoals are likely to be laterally extensive, forming sheet-like units where the system is prograding,
but with local thickening of the shoals where aggradation has dominated. These conditions developed
during the highstand systems tract when sedimentation was keeping pace with base level rise
(aggradation)  or beginning  to outpace base  level  rise causing  progradation due  to  lack  of
accommodation space.

This facies association is best developed in cores 6, 7, 8 and 9. In cores 6 and 7 the shoal complex is
over 24m thick and consists of aggrading OGl and OIGl lithofacies throughout the main shoal
shoreface and interior. Towards the top of the sequence, when accommodation space was reduced,
the backshoal comprises more thinly interbedded OGl, IGl and PGl lithofacies units. In cores 8 and 9
the shoal complex dominates with sequences of 13 and 22m thickness. Between 3188m and 3201m
the barrier shoal is well developed, particularly the shoreface (BSc) and shoal interior (BSb) and is
overlain by thin lagoonal sediments and an exposure surface. Above here the aggrading shoal is re-
established with very thin, minor flooding of subtidal foreshoal FSa lithofacies (transgressive events)
throughout the shoal’s development.

3.3.2.1 Burrowed shoal shoreface (BSd)

The burrowed shoal shoreface was deposited below the fairweather wave base and possibly below
storm wavebase, as moderate energy, relatively deep, open marine shoals.

The burrowed shoal (BSd) is dominated by coarse, bioturbated and locally wispy laminated peloidal
and oolitic grainstones and packstones (OGd, OGl, PGd, PGl) with minor dolomitised relict grain
grainstones (RGd) and mudstones.


                                    Page 15 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 24 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


3.3.2.2 Shoal shoreface (BSc)

The shoal shoreface (BSc) depositional facies was deposited at/above the fairweather wave base in a
high energy environment.  This depositional facies is typically composed of medium to coarse
grained, planar and trough cross-bedded, oolitic, bioclastic and peloidal grainstones (OGd, OGl, BGl,
PGl, PGd), with common coarse, intraclastic basal lags (IPGd) and rare thin mudstones (MWd). The
shoal shoreface sediments are commonly stacked to form thick, porous and permeable units tens of
metres thick.

3.3.2.3 Barrier shoal interior (BSb)

The barrier shoal itself was deposited in high energy conditions above fairweather wave base as the
shoal interior (BSb).

The oolitic grainstone and packstone (OGl, OGd, OIGl, OIGd) and some of the peloidal and
bioclastic grainstone and packstone lithofacies (PGl, PGd, BGl, BGd), which dominate the main
shoal (BSb), were deposited in a high energy current and/or wave agitated environment above
fairweather wave base within a barrier shoal complex or foreshoal beach deposit. Conditions for the
formation of ooid dominated grainstones and packstones were shallow waters where temperature and
salinity were high enough to cause precipitation and growth of the grains. A certain degree of
restriction of the environment is also required to promote the elevation of salinity by evaporation.
The partial restriction of this environment is reflected in the fauna present in the BGl and BGd
lithofacies where gastropods, miliolid foraminifera and green algae are abundant.   Tidal  belt
marginal to shallow restricted lagoons and ramp/platform interiors are ideal settings for ooid
formation (Tucker and Wright, 1990). Onshore directed, planar cross bedding is strongly indicative
of this shallow, wave dominated facies association.

3.3.2.4 Barrier backshoal (BSa)

The backshoal (Bsa) facies was deposited in slightly more protected, moderate energy conditions
behind the main shoal (Figure 2).

The backshoal sediments (BSa) are generally more strongly peloidal (PGd, PGl) with some ooid
packstones (OGl, OGd) and common mudstones and wackestones (MWd, MWl).  The peloidal
lithofacies are likely to have formed by micritisation of pre-existing ooids by endolithic micro-
organisms (Bathurst, 1975) inferring a longer residence time on the sea-floor prior to final burial.
The backshoal sediments are commonly characterised by being relatively thinly bedded and more
strongly laminated than the rest of the shoal complex, with common restricted fauna such as
gastropods and miliolids.  The common mudstone and wackestone lithofacies suggest a quieter
environment of deposition behind the shoal which developed as accommodation space diminished
during highstand.

3.3.3  Lagoon (L)

The  subtidal environment  described  here was formed towards  late  highstand  conditions  as
sedimentation was outpacing waning base level rise. The environment developed as accommodation
space was  diminishing and  water ponded  behind  the  barrier  shoal  complex. Low  energy
environments prevailed because of protection by the offshore shoal complex (Figure 2). Water
restriction was variably affected by the efficiency of the shoal complex providing a barrier to open
marine circulation. Sedimentary structures and fossil assemblages indicate both open and restricted

                                    Page 16 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 25 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


environments occurred. Communication to the open ocean was via channels breaching the barrier or
perhaps the barrier may have been incomplete periodically through deposition.

Within the open lagoon (Lo), interbedded grain dominated and mud dominated sediments were
deposited in moderate energy conditions. The restricted lagoon (Lr) environment was of lower
energy and dominated by mud supported sediments including mottled or locally bioturbated to
vaguely laminated, organic rich mudstones. The open lagoonal facies association comprises three
depositional facies: the thrombolitic algal facies (La) which exhibits distinct domal, columnar or
vertically established convolute algal structures on a centimetre scale with internal lamination; shoal
spillovers (Lb) occurring as thin, poorly formed OGd/OGl lithofacies within the open lagoonal
MWd/MWl lithofacies, deposited on the shoal side of the lagoon and tidal channel aprons (Lc) which
formed on the landward, tidal flat, side of the lagoon, characterised by high angle cross laminated,
thin PGl and OGl sub-lithofacies units within the lagoonal mudstones and wackestones (Table 7).

The environment of deposition envisaged for the thrombolitic (La) structures was probably one of a
shallow subtidal setting with some tidal and wave turbulence as suggested by the grainstone and
packstone textures. It could be that the thrombolitic structures acted as baffles and reduced current
energy which caused deposition of the grainstones and packstones in between. Alternatively, the
thrombolites were responding to rapid deposition and shifting substrate and the growth form is a
result of the organism ‘keeping up’ with sediment accumulation. Either way, the water depth was
probably a little greater than the other stromatolite growth areas, such as the tidal flats, and was
nearer to the influence of wave and tidal activities.  Thrombolitic algal stromatolites occur in
association with other stromatolite types as they grew in response to the changing base levels and
may form part of shallowing cycles.

Of relatively minor importance were the formation of oncolitic grainstones and packstones of the
ONGd sub-lithofacies (Table 5). The oncoids represent well lit, shallow water to allow the growth of
algae around a nuclei often comprising gastropod and bivalve fragments. Constant but gentle wave
agitation  is also a  pre-requisite of concentric growth around the nuclei.  Within the lagoon
environment, gastropod rich peloidal packstones and mudstones were also deposited in these
restricted, relatively quiet environments where micritisation of grains could take place and there was
abundant algae present for grazing gastropods.

3.3.4   Tidal Flat (TF)

Deposited within the intertidal zone, the tidal flats occurred landward of the lagoons but seaward of
the supratidal sabkha zone (Figure  2). Gradual progradation of the  flats occurred during late
highstand as accommodation space was minimal because base  level  rise was low. Sediment
production therefore outpaced sea level rise. Current and tide energy were low resulting in the
deposition of the mudstones, wackestones and packstones as the tidal flats were periodically flooded
during high tide.

The daily exposure and elevated salinity produced a harsh environment which was not conducive to
the development of an active benthos, and burrowing and bioturbation were minimal. The tidal flats
often became brecciated, due to desiccation breaking up the surface, during exposure and subsequent
flooding especially during storms.

The tidal flat facies association has been subdivided into six depositional facies: storm washovers
(TFf), algal mats (TFd), tidal channels (TFc) and the intertidal flats (TFe), which form the true
intertidal facies, combined with the high intertidal evaporitic pools or salinas (TFb) and supratidal
sabkha (TFa) which are more frequently exposed.

                                    Page 17 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 26 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id



3.3.4.1 The depositional facies of the tidal flat

The intertidal flats (TFe) are dominated by strongly laminated mudstones with common grainy
laminae. In the lower intertidal environment and at the edges of evaporitic ponds, stromatolitic algal
lamination may start to form, whereas in the high intertidal, rootlets and fenestrae are indicative of
local plant colonisation and ruptured laminae/tepee structures suggest an even shallower, commonly
exposed environment. Intraclastic packstone intervals of locally derived rip-up clasts were deposited
during storm events (TFf) and display a wide range of lithofacies including: OIGd, IPGd, MWd,
RFMd and BGd. The TFf facies generally form thin packstone laminae within the intertidal flats.

The algal mats (TFd) are characterised by filamentous cryptalgal laminated boundstones (LSBd,
LSBl, RFMd)  with  thin  entrapped  packstones  or  grainstones  (PGd). The  different  growth
morphologies of the algal structures are a consequence of environmental conditions such as water
depth and current/wave energy. In general terms, the laminated stromatolitic algae (LSBl/LSBd)
grew on a low energy tidal flat or on the sheltered edges of lagoons and evaporitic ponds.

Tidal channels (TFc) locally cross-cut the flats and are generally higher energy features than most of
the  intertidal  flat.  The  tidal channels are characterised by relatively thin, sharply delineated
packstone and grainstone units with common pebbly basal lags and trough cross-lamination, and are
generally thinly (up to 0.5m) interbedded with the tidal flat laminated mudstones. The meandering
nature of the channels results in sheet geometry.

Storm washovers (TFf) comprising packstones and grainstones with sheet geometry, are composed of
sediments derived from intertidal and subtidal environments washed onto the tidal flats during high
energy events such as storms. Sediments found in the barrier shoal complex occur, such as oolitic
and peloidal lithofacies (Tables 5 and 7).

The salina and evaporitic pools depositional facies (TFb) comprises chevron and palisade (AP) and
laminated and nodular (AL) anhydrite sub-lithofacies. The anhydrites formed from the surface
evaporation of hypersaline shallow ponds and pools developed on the sabkha during very late
highstand or even lowstand conditions. It is envisaged that this mode of formation may result in thick
anhydrite intervals where the pools are regularly replenished by washover waters and may be
associated with thin algal mat interbeds which formed at the pool edges. The salinas and evaporitic
pool anhydrites are characterised by sharp basal contacts where the evaporites form on the bottom of
the pond with sharp top contacts where transgression occurs.  As such, this facies association
commonly represents the top of a stratigraphic cycle, and is typically overlain by a transgressive lag
and a return to more open marine or shoal barrier sedimentation.

The  supratidal sabkha depositional  facies (TFa) predominantly comprises chickenwire mosaic
anhydrite (AC) and mottled anhydrite (AM)  sub-lithofacies.  It  represents the  final phase of
shallowing associated with deposition of the stratigraphic cycles and occurred during late highstand
conditions. Formation of the anhydrite was within a supratidal environment occurring above mean
high tide level in arid conditions. These conditions caused strong net evaporation and surface pore
waters became highly concentrated and drawn to the surface causing precipitation at the water table
of diagenetic minerals, the most important of which was anhydrite (section 5.2.3). The anhydrite
generally replaced most of the original sediment, with only thin stringers of the original host rock
present, comprising peloid/pellet packstones and lime wackestones (PGl and MWl sub-lithofacies).

                                                    Reservoir Geological Study of Cores from the
                            SP-9 Well (Kangan and Dalan Formations), South Pars Field, Iran


                                      CHAPTER 4


                    Sequence Stratigraphy and the Depositional Model



4.1   INTRODUCTION

The sequence stratigraphic interpretation shown in this study is based upon the identification of large
and medium scale cyclicity and associated progradation, aggradation and retrogradation of the
foreshoal, barrier shoal, lagoon and tidal  flat sediments.  The boundaries identified (Table 9,
Enclosures 1-8) are based upon a framework of sediment distribution generated by sea level
fluctuations.  The reservoir layer boundaries identified by TFE and amended by RRI, using core
calibration, are shown in Table 10. The sequence stratigraphic boundaries have been identified from
wireline log analysis and from calibration with detailed sedimentological study of core.  The
sequences boundaries identified represent the top surfaces of stacked, genetically related packages of
sediment within a time correlative framework.

In order to avoid repetition, the sequence stratigraphic model used in this report is based upon the
model previously generated by TotalFinaElf for the South Pars Field. TFE identified sequence
stratigraphic cycles on various scales and used a nomenclature based upon the stacking patterns and
the position relative to the terminal shelf-margin of the preceding sequence (Posamentier and Vail,
1988). TFE attributed a series of tops in comparison to SP-5 to enable the identification of bounding
surfaces on a semi-regional scale. In this report this model has been followed, as far as possible, to
enable integration and correlation with the aforementioned study.

The sequence stratigraphic cycles within the succession were identified from core and wireline log
patterns by defining lithofacies, sub-lithofacies and facies associations and assessing their stacking
patterns. Depositional modelling of the facies associations gives an idea of probable aerial extent and
geometry of the component sediments. The identification of shallowing upwards or deepening
upwards intervals defined larger scale cycles, which were also identifiable from wireline logs, was
used to define the sequence stratigraphic framework. The large scale cycles generally exhibit
shallowing upwards trends from transgression to highstand, which is indicated on the data summary
charts (Enclosures 1-8).

The principles of carbonate sequence stratigraphy have not been described in detail as it is assumed
the reader has a basic understanding.  For a full discussion see Sarg (1988), Schlager (1991),
Handford and Loucks (1994) and Kendall and Schlager (1981).

4.2   REGIONAL GEOLOGICAL SETTING

The present day Arabian Plate Basin (sensu Sharland et al., 2001) is asymmetric. To the west it is
bounded by the exposed Precambrian Arabian Shield, which was uplifted in the Late Oligocene by
the Red Sea, and Gulf of Aden rift system. The basin deepens gently in an easterly direction with a

                                    Page 19 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 28 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


maximum depth reached in a foredeep setting in front of the Zagros collision zone (Konert et al.,
2001).

In the late Early Permian, the Arabian Plate (sensu Sharland et al., 2001) is interpreted to have
undergone a major phase of crustal extension resulting in continental separation. Progressive thermal
uplift is interpreted to have occurred along the present-day Zagros Suture culminating in the regional
‘pre Khuff unconformity’ (Sharland et al., 2001). In the Late Permian, continental spreading took
place as the Neo-Tethys Ocean started to form.

During this time the Dalan Formation transgressed the pre-Dalan clastics of the Furghun Formation
(initiating the tectonostratigraphic megasequence AP6 of Sharland et al., 2001). Movement on major
faults in Abu Dhabi produced substantial, thickness variations within the lower Dalan. Movement on
these faults ceased, however, following the deposition of the base middle anhydrite (maximum
flooding surface P30, Sharland et al., 2001). Major shallow water carbonate platform sedimentation
subsequently prevailed across the entire Arabian Plate, lasting until the late Toarcian (Sharland et al.,
2001).

Short-term sea level oscillations caused cyclic shoaling pulses within the Dalan (Ziegler, 2001). As a
result, the succession consists of numerous, stacked shallowing-upwards cyclothems, each 2m-10m
thick. There is stratigraphic variation in the character of the cycles, in the Kangan most are oolite-
dominated  at the base and grade upwards  into laminates and sabkha anhydrites  at the  top.
Cyclothems are arranged into larger cyclothems, on several orders of scale in stacking patterns that
indicate transgressive and regressive events (Reading, 1996)

4.3   SEQUENCE STRATIGRAPHIC FRAMEWORK

By adopting the TFE framework, the stratigraphic interval in SP-9 has the following sequence
hierarchy:

-    Small-scale depositional cycles. High frequency metre scale single sequences with a duration
    assumed to be in the order of 0.01 to 0.1 Ma (?fifth order).

-    Depositional units. Stacked small-scale depositional cycles forming cycle sets, e.g. KS4a-1
    assumed to be in the order 0.1 to 1.0 Ma duration. (?fourth order).

-    Depositional  sequences. Deposited  during a  single complete  cycle  of  sea  level change
    demonstrating retrogradational, transgressive and aggradational to progradational process in
    response to base level change and accommodation space. They correspond to: KS4a, KS4b,
    KS3a, KS3b, KS2, KS1a and KS1b with a duration assumed to be in the order of 1.0 to 5.0 Ma
     (? third order).

-   Major sequences. The Kangan and Dalan Formations are divided into four major sequences KS1
     to KS4 which are composed internally of depositional sequences, for example KS4 is made up
     of KS4a and KS4b depositional sequences.

The definition of depositional cycles may mean that they do not necessarily correspond to important
reservoir layers as no account is taken of diagenetic overprinting which are discussed in Section 5.3
and integrated in Section 6. Each depositional sequence is described below in order of deposition
starting with the K4 major sequence.

                                    Page 20 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 29 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


4.3.1  KS4 Major Sequence

The KS4 comprises the KS4a and KS4b depositional sequences.

4.3.1.1 KS4a depositional sequence

Average Porosity = 3.5%                   Average Permeability = 25.5 mD

The KS4a depositional sequence is 57.25m long at the base of the core and comprises 6 depositional
units (KS4a-1 to KS4a-6) of similar thicknesses, which themselves may be composed of 1 or more
small-scale depositional cycles.

Depositional Lithofacies

The sequence  is  entirely dolomitised and  is dominated by peloidal and ooid packstones and
grainstones (PGd, OGd sub-lithofacies), mudstones and wackestones (MWd, RFMd sub-lithofacies)
and boundstones (LSBd, TBd sub-lithofacies) lithofacies.  The sequence comprises a series of
generally fining upwards cycles reflecting shallowing from foreshoal mudstones, through high
energy coarser grained barrier shoal sediments to finer tidal flat and lagoonal sediments. This is
typical of the depositional units, which demonstrate 6 shallowing events (Enclosure 8).

The base of the sequence is dominated by dolomitised mudstones, relict grain grainstones and
relatively thick sequences of displacive anhydrite (KS4a-1, 2 depositional units). The middle part of
the sequence (KS4a-3, 4, 5 and base 6 depositional units) predominantly comprises grain dominated
PGd and OGd sub-lithofacies in thinly bedded units (~0.5m) with common pebbly basal lags. Ooids,
peloids, coated grains, benthonic foraminifera and gastropods are present scattered throughout the
interval, with gastropods becoming more common towards the top of the interval. The uppermost
part of KS4a-6, at the top of KS4a, is dominated by dolomitised rootlet/fenestral mudstones (RFMd)
and dolomitised laminated stromatolitic boundstones (LSBd) with rare thrombolitic boundstones
(TBd).

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

The lowermost boundary of the KS4a-1 depositional unit is with the underlying anhydrite of the Nar
Formation, which represents sabkha conditions. This probable Type 1 sequence boundary is overlain
by wackestones of the foreshoal depositional facies (FSa) representing the transgressive systems
tract. Three small scale depositional cycles are present which shallow through highstand barrier
shoals to well developed intertidal flat with late highstand anhydritic evaporitic pools and minor
sabkhas. The uppermost boundary of KS4a-1 is marked by the presence of a rootlet horizon of the
RFMd sub-lithofacies indicating exposure at this boundary (Type 1 SB). In comparison with SP-5
and -6, this unit has a thicker development in SP-9.

The KSa-2 depositional unit is composed of one shallowing cycle from grainstone shoal deposits
representing the transgressive systems tract (which overlie the rootlet horizon) through restricted
lagoon environments to tidal flats with sabkhas and evaporitic pools. The uppermost boundary is
marked by a surface with desiccation features such as tepee structures. The thickness of this unit is
consistent with other wells.

KS4a-3, 4, 5 and base 6 depositional units comprise a series of shallowing upwards cycles from thin,
poorly developed, middle shelf foreshoal mudstones, through well developed, but thinly bedded,
dolomitised  barrier  shoals capped by generally  thin, poorly developed lagoon and  tidal  flat

                                    Page 21 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 30 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


sediments. The boundaries of the units are marked by facies  shifts, typically tidal  flat facies
association sediments overlain by grainstones of the barrier shoal complex, indicating a flooding
transgressive event as base level rose. The top of the KS4a-6 (and top part of KS4a) is characterised
by well developed thrombolitic algal depositional facies (La) of the lagoonal facies association.

4.3.1.2 KS4b depositional sequence

Average Porosity = 16.7%                  Average Permeability = 191.8 mD

The KS4b depositional sequence is 103.1m thick and comprises 6 depositional units (KS4b-1 to
KS4b-6) of very variable thicknesses (range: 4.15m to 38.71m), which themselves may be composed
of 1 or more smaller scale cycles.

Depositional Lithofacies

This depositional sequence is dominated by well developed, stacked ooid packstones and grainstone
packages of the barrier shoal facies association, with a relatively thin interval of boundstone and
mudstone lithofacies. The sequence comprises a series of generally fining upwards cycles reflecting
shallowing from foreshoal mudstones (transgressive), through well developed, high energy coarser
grained barrier shoal sediments (aggrading highstand) to less well developed tidal flat and lagoonal
sediments. Late highstand sabkha and evaporitic ponds are not frequently developed. This is typical
of the depositional units, which demonstrate 6 shallowing events (Enclosures 5, 6 and 7).

The base of the sequence (KS4b-1 and base KS4b-2) is dominated by dolomitised boundstones
(LSBd, TBd) and mudstones (MWd) derived from both relatively shallow and relatively deep
environments.

The majority of the depositional sequence (KS4b-2, 3, 4) predominantly comprises thick intervals of
ooid or peloidal and coated grain dominated lithofacies with little variation in grain size except for a
tendency to fine upwards and become muddier at the top of each individual unit.  These mud
supported intervals become thicker upwards. The  oolitic and peloidal intervals are commonly
massive, but may also exhibit planar to low angle lamination with rare trough cross-bedding and
current  ripples.   Indeterminate burrows or burrow mottling are common in  distinct  intervals,
commonly underlying more strongly laminated units.  Thin intraclastic units are common, as are
pebbly lags to individual ooid beds.  Throughout this interval a variety of open marine to more
restricted fauna are common, including benthonic foraminifera, gastropods and miliolids.

The uppermost  part  of KS4b-4 and KS4b-5  is dominated by mudstones and wackestones
(MWl/MWd) with common planar lamination, horizontal bioturbation and abundant miliolids.
Overlying  the mudstones  is a massive  anhydrite and a  series  of  relatively well developed
stromatolitic and thrombolitic boundstones.  KS4b-6 is characterised by variably bioturbated and
laminated, relatively thinly bedded, peloidal and peloidal intraclastic packstones and grainstones
(PGd/IPGd).

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

KS4b-1, depositional unit is composed of late highstand lagoon to high intertidal boundstones with a
transgressive flooding event at the top of KS4b-1 which is overlain by a transgressive sheet lag and a
return to open marine, middle shelf foreshoal mudstones as sea level rose. The thickness of this unit
is consistent with other wells.

The KS3a depositional sequence is 67.36m in thickness and comprises 7 depositional units (KS3a-1
to KS4a-7), ranging from 3.44m to 19.92m thick. These are composed of 1 or more small-scale
depositional cycles. The depositional units have similar thickness in other wells.

Depositional Lithofacies

This sequence is characterised by a high proportion of mudstones and wackestones with infrequent
anhydrite intervals.  Peloidal, peloidal intraclastic and relict grain packstones and grainstones are
only thinly developed, interbedded with the thick mudstone intervals.  The sequence comprises a
series of variable thickness, fining upwards cycles reflecting shallowing from foreshoal mudstones,
through poorly developed high energy coarser grained barrier shoal sediments to finer, but well
developed lagoonal, tidal flat and evaporitic pool/sabkha sediments.

The base of the sequence (KS3a-1 and 2) is composed of dolomitised mudstones, peloidal and relict
grain grainstones with  relatively thick sequences of palisade anhydrite.  The mudstones and
packstones/grainstones are commonly bioturbated (indeterminate and Zoophycos burrows) and are
locally strongly planar laminated with rare low angle cross lamination in the grainstones. The middle
part of the sequence (KS3a-3, 4 and 5) predominantly comprises thick (up to 5m) intervals of
mudstones and wackestones with thin (0.5m-1m), intercalated, fining upwards, peloidal, ooid and
bioclastic grainstones and extremely thin (<10 cm) organic rich mudstones.  Locally, massive to
nodular anhydrite is developed. A variety of fauna are present within this interval, dominated by
green algae and benthonic foraminifera with  rare bryozoa.   Grain dominated PGd and OGd
lithofacies are present in thinly bedded units (~0.5m) with common pebbly basal lags. KS3a-5, 6 and
7 have a higher percentage of packstones and grainstones with locally well developed, thinly
interbedded, high angle cross-bedded peloidal (PGd, IPGd), ooid (OGl) and relict grain (RGl)
grainstones with intraclastic basal lags. The mudstones are more poorly developed with common
fenestral structures (RFMd), bioturbation and local desiccation.

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

The KS3a facies analysis shows  it to be composed of a series of generally shallowing upwards
sequences, from middle shelf foreshoal to tidal flat.

KS3a-1 and 2 depositional units are composed of several small scale depositional cycles of foreshoal,
barrier shoal, and restricted lagoon with well developed evaporitic pools/salina anhydrites suggesting
the development of significant evaporitic tidal flats. The facies shift at the boundaries of KS3a-1 and
2 indicate Type 1 sequence boundaries.

A major flooding event (transgressive systems tract) at the top of KS3a-2 marks the start of a period
dominated by deeper  sedimentation with  well developed  foreshoal and  barrier  shoal  facies
associations.

The KS3a-3, 4 and 5 depositional units are dominated by sub-tidal shelf mudstones with common
shoal spillovers of grainy sediments on to the foreshoal.  Throughout this middle KS3a interval,
poorly developed barrier shoal, lagoon and intertidal flats are developed, indicative of the continued
presence of shallowing upwards cycles.

The upper section of the KS3a sequence represents a return to shallower sedimentation, being
dominated by peloidal shoal grainstone shoreface and back barrier shoals and intertidal flats with
well developed tidal channels.

                                    Page 24 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 33 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


4.3.2.2 KS3b depositional sequence

Average Porosity = 5.3%                   Average Permeability = 26.0 mD

The KS3b sequence  is 34.61m long and comprises 6 depositional units (KS3b-1 to KS3b-6),
generally of similar thickness (range: 3.88m to 10.9m). Each unit is composed of 1 or more small-
scale depositional cycles. The depositional units have similar thickness in other wells.

Depositional Lithofacies

This sequence is dominated by peloidal and bioclastic packstones and grainstones (PGd, BGd sub-
lithofacies) intercalated with mudstones and wackestones (MWd). The KS3b sequence can be split
into a lower and an upper interval, representing two large sub-cycles.

The lower interval, KS3b-1 to KS3b-4, consists of a series of stacked, generally fining upwards
sediments dominated by dolomitised mudstones and wackestones interbedded with relatively thin
packstone and grainstones. The mudstones are typically mottled with indeterminate burrowing and
rare, poorly developed laminae or desiccated surfaces. The top of the interval (KS3b-4) is marked by
a  well developed  cryptalgally laminated boundstone.  The  better developed packstones and
grainstones are commonly intraclastic and fine upwards with planar to low angle cross-lamination,
defined by grainy laminae. A wide range of fauna are present, from more restricted miliolids and
gastropods to more open marine green algae and benthonic foraminifera.

The upper interval, KS3b-5 and KS3b-6, comprises a stacked series of thinly interbedded, generally
fining upwards peloidal packstones and grainstones (PGd) and mudstones and wackestones (MWd).
The mudstones, wackestones, packstones and grainstones are generally massive, with only rare
lamination or bioturbation. The faunal association seen in this part of the sequence is predominantly
open marine with rare gastropods indicating local restriction.

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

Analysis of the KS3b facies shows it to be composed of a series of generally shallowing upwards
sequences, from middle shelf foreshoal to intertidal flats.

KS3b-1, 2, 3 and 4 comprise four shallowing upwards depositional units which combine to form a
larger scale shallowing upwards cycle. At the base of the interval the foreshoal and barrier shoal
facies are predominant with relatively poorly developed, quickly flooded lagoon and tidal  flat
sediments. Upwards, the lagoon and intertidal flat sediments become more strongly developed with
increasingly short-lived open marine sedimentation after each transgressive event.

KS3b-5 commences with a major transgressive flooding event, drowning the well developed
intertidal algal flat, beginning a return to generally more open marine sedimentation. KS3b-5 and 6
comprise two, relatively thin shallowing upwards cycles from foreshoal to tidal flat, interrupted by a
partially developed shoal sub-cycle.  Comprehensive flooding of the intertidal flat at the top of
KS3b-6 marks the top of the sequence.

4.3.3  KS2 Major Sequence

Average Porosity = 10.0%                  Average Permeability = 83.4 mD

                                    Page 25 r
