The KS2 sequence is 68.18m long and comprises 5 depositional units of varying thickness (range:
5.16m to 29.63m), identified by TFE. However, RRI postulate a further boundary at 2977.9m (KS2-
3ii). Each cycle is composed of 1 or more smaller scale cycles. The depositional units have similar
thickness in other wells.

Depositional Lithofacies

This sequence is dominated by peloidal and bioclastic packstones and grainstones (PGd, BGd sub-
lithofacies) intercalated with mudstones and wackestones (MWd). The KS3b depositional sequence
can be split into a lower and an upper interval, representing two large cycles.

KS2-1 is dominated by mottled or cryptalgally laminated mudstones and wackestones with thin,
poorly developed, massive ooid and relict grain packstones and grainstones.  KS2-2 and 3ii are
dominated by massive, mottled, bioturbated and planar to low angle cross laminated ooid and relict
grain grainstones. The uppermost part of the interval marks the Permian-Triassic boundary and is
strongly cryptalgally laminated with local thrombolites.

KS2-3i and KS2-4 comprise a thick series of massive, grainy/pebbly ooid and ooid intraclastic
grainstones topped by relict grain grainstones and a thin cryptalgal laminated boundstone. Very thin,
laminated mudstones are intercalated with the ooid grainstones.  The uppermost cycle in this
sequence (KS2-5) comprises thin, planar laminated ooid grainstones interbedded with bioturbated
mudstones, overlain by nodular and soft sediment deformed anhydrite with thin intercalations of
cryptalgally laminated boundstone.

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

Analysis of the KS2 facies shows it to be composed of a series of generally shallowing upwards
sequences, from middle shelf foreshoal to intertidal flats with a massive barrier shoal development
mid-sequence.   Overall, the KS2 sequence can be viewed as a generally shallowing upwards
sequence with the lower part of KS2 dominated by open marine sedimentation, the mid-section by
barrier shoals and the top dominated by intertidal flats and anhydrites. The boundary with the
underlying KS3 is marked by a significant facies shift from tidal flat facies association of the top of
KS3 to the overlying shoal grainstones of the KS2 transgressive systems tract.

KS2-1 and KS2-2 depositional units are dominated by barrier shoal sediments deposited during
periods of highstand by sediment aggradation, with  cyclic flooding represented by foreshoal
sediments and lagoonal sediments forming during late highstand.

The cryptalgal lamination  at the top of KS2-3ii  is interpreted to be the result of significant
development of tidal flat and shallow lagoon sediments during late highstand.

KS2-3i basal contact (Transgressive Surface) shows that flooding of the tidal flat occurred, with the
deposition of thick shallowing upwards barrier shoal grainstones. This was followed by periods of
poorly developed lagoons and tidal flats, succeeded by flooding and a return to shoal sedimentation
(KS2-3i, 4).  Development of the late highstand systems tract  is represented by the return to
intertidal, cryptalgal stromatolites and evaporative pond anhydrites at the top of KS2-4.

The KS2-5 transgression occurred with the deposition of shoal sediments. This was followed by
thick tidal flat boundstones and mudstones, and finally evaporitic pool anhydrites during highstand
and late highstand conditions.

4.3.4  KS1 Major Sequence

The KS1 comprises the KS1a and KS1b depositional sequences.

4.3.4.1 KS1a depositional sequence

Average Porosity = 4.4%                   Average Permeability = 7.5 mD

The KS1a is 42.87m long and comprises depositional units (KS1a-1 to KS1a-3) of varying thickness
(range: 6.06m to 24.19m). Each cycle is composed of 1 or more small-scale depositional cycles.

Depositional Lithofacies

This  depositional sequence  is dominated by  thinly interbedded boundstones, mudstones and
wackestones (LSBd, TBd, MWl, MWd sub-lithofacies) and peloidal, ooid, ooid intraclastic and
bioclastic packstones and grainstones (PGd, OGd, OIGd, BGd).

KS1a-1  is  fining upwards  cycle,  similar  to the underlying KS2-5 sediments.  A  thin basal
transgressive mudstone is overlain by moderately thin, bioturbated and trough cross-laminated ooid
and bioclastic packstones with abundant open marine to slightly restricted biota.   Planar and
cryptalgal laminated and locally brecciated mudstones and boundstones, intercalated with nodular
and laminated anhydrite represent the late highstand at the top of the cycle.

KS1a-2 and KS1a-3 comprise a series of very thin, rapidly evolving cycles and part-cycles.  The
lower part of KS1a-2 comprises slightly thicker interbedded laminated mudstones and wackestones
(MWd) and planar  to low angle cross-laminated,  fine grained ooid and peloidal packstones.
Upsequence, the ooid grainstones (OGd/OGl) are thinly intercalated with cryptalgal boundstones
(LSBd) and ooid intraclastic grainstones (OIGd), with local oncoidal grainstones (ONGd) and
nodular and palisade anhydrite.  KS1a-3 is dominated by thin, wispy laminated and mottled ooid,
peloidal and bioclastic grainstones with thin interbeds of planar to cryptalgal laminated mudstones
and wackestones.

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

KS1a depositional sequence shows a series of short, thin, generally shallowing upwards sequences,
from middle shelf foreshoal to tidal flat facies association.

The KS2 sequence boundary is marked by flooding (Transgressive Surface) and the resumption of
open marine foreshoal sedimentation with rapid shallowing to the foreshoal and main shoal. It is a
Type 1 SB. The sequence continues to shallow upwards into a lagoon, with at least partial restriction,
and a well developed tidal flat facies association. Sabkha anhydrites have formed within the intertidal
flats and evaporitic ponds periodically formed on the high intertidal flat forming salina anhydrites.
The periodic, prolonged exposure of the high intertidal surface resulted in desiccation of the surface.

The top of the KS1a-1 depositional unit is marked by a sharp erosional flooding surface and the
deposition of transgressive barrier shoal grainstones.

During KS1a-2 minor periodic flooding has resulted in two short, shallowing upwards sequences
from foreshoal (FS) to main shoal (BS), followed by the development of a more extensive barrier
shoal and thick lagoon sequence during highstand. The formation of an intertidal flat at the top of
KS1a-2 was periodically interrupted by minor flooding back to a lagoon environment.

The base of KS1a-3 is marked by a flood back and facies shift to foreshoal environment followed by
a shallowing to shoal and tidal flats. Minor transgression resulted in a return to dominant barrier
shoal sedimentation with rare intertidal flat or lagoon development.

4.3.4.2 KS1b depositional sequence

Average Porosity = 6.7%                   Average Permeability = 25.2 mD

The KS1b sequence  is 35.8m long and 2 depositional units (KS1b-1 and KS1b-2) of varying
thickness (range: 10.82m to 29.98m) were observed in the cored SP-9 (KS1b-3 has not been cored in
this well). Each unit is composed of 1 or more small-scale depositional cycles.

Depositional Lithofacies

This sequence is dominated by thinly interbedded mudstones and wackestones (MWl, MWd sub-
lithofacies) and peloidal and ooid packstones and grainstones (PGd, OGd) with thin laminated
boundstones (LSBd).

The base of KS1b-1 comprises thinly interbedded, strongly laminated mudstones, and variably
laminated, locally current rippled peloidal and ooid grainstones at the base. The mudstone sediments
become rarer upwards with increasingly thicker, mottled to laminated oolitic grainstones. Towards
the top of the unit mudstones and boundstones become more common again with thrombolitic and
cryptalgal structures. The base of KS1b-2 is marked by a return to fine grained bioclastic and ooid
grainstones, fining upwards to a desiccated mudstone surface before coarsening upwards to a
strongly bioturbated mudstone at the top of the cored interval.

Depositional Environment/Facies Associations

The KS1b-1 depositional unit shows three small scale depositional cycles, shallowing upwards with
locally well developed barrier shoal sediments.

The basal transgressive systems tract is present as a thin shoal, overlain by a thick, variably restricted
lagoon with thin tidal channels and shoal spillovers into the lagoon developed during highstand when
accommodation space was minimal. The second small scale depositional cycle shows a transgressive
flooding event with the onset of ooid shoal sedimentation in a shallowing environment, resulting in
the deposition of a strongly laminated intertidal flat at the top of the cycle.  Finally, there was a
further flooding and a return to shoal sedimentation, which again shallowed upwards to a lagoon and
tidal flat environment. The boundary at the top of KS1b-1 is marked by a facies shift from a tidal flat
facies association to shoal facies association demonstrating the flooding of the next transgressive
systems tract of KS1b-2.

Only the lower part of KS1b-2 was cored in SP-9, the base is marked by a transgression with shoal
sedimentation shallowing up to a tidal flat to the end of the first of two small scale depositional
cycles. Flooding then occurred as the next small scale depositional cycle was deposited.

4.4   SUMMARY OF THE DEPOSITIONAL HISTORY

The hierarchy of depositional cyclicity in SP-9 conforms to the sequence stratigraphic framework
proposed by TFE. Third order depositional sequences have been identified from the stacking patterns
of depositional units and small-scale depositional cycles by detailed facies modelling. The typical
sequence composing the depositional units is a middle shelf facies association deposited below fair
weather wave base shallowing to a tidal flat environment often with the development of evaporitic
pools and the precipitation of anhydrite. Diagenetic anhydrite may also be associated with supratidal
sabkha deposits.

The transgressive systems tract of the depositional units often comprises the middle shelf facies
association suggesting a rapid deepening or rapid base level change with concomitant increase in
available accommodation space. A slower sea level response causes the deposition of grainstones of
the shoal facies association in shallow water, high energy conditions. The highstand systems tract is
typified by the deposition of grainstones of the barrier shoal complex as shallow water conditions
favourable for ooid formation came into existence. Aggradation and the development of thick,
stacked grainstone packages occurred as base level rise and ooid/sediment generation were in
equilibrium. Outward progradation occurred as sedimentation outpaced base level rise. Lagoons
developed behind the barrier shoal complex, with the degree to which water circulation was
restricted being a function of the effectiveness of the shoal complex to behave as a barrier. Discrete
grainstone beds are observed, interbedded with fine grained, low energy lagoonal sediments, and
represent reworking of shoal complex sediment into the lagoon. Often the lagoonal environment is
poorly developed in the core and is overlain by better developed tidal flat sediments. The tidal flat
environment is represented by a variety of depositional facies reflecting subtle changes in base level
and accommodation space. Supratidal conditions signify the most extreme shallowing within a
depositional unit and are represented by anhydrite, either precipitated from saline pools or within the
tidal flat sediments themselves by net evaporation in arid conditions. Often depositional units are
capped by intertidal flat sediments or algal mat boundstones, which overlie anhydrite. This may
suggest that the anhydrite formed within the tidal flat of algal mats or that the base level turnaround
point had been reached and the transgressive systems tract of the next depositional unit had started. If
the latter is the case then the depositional unit will terminate at the top of the anhydrite layer.

On the field scale, sediment geometry appears to be relatively consistent, for example the stacked
grainstones of KS4b-4 have a similar thickness in SP-1, 6 and 5. Similarly, the tidal flat dominated
depositional units of KS3b and KS3a appear to be consistent in these wells. It is probable that the
facies associations in SP-9 have sheet-like geometry occurring over the inter-well areas.





                                    Page 29 r

[ صفحه 38 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────

                                                    Reservoir Geological Study of Cores from the
                            SP-9 Well (Kangan and Dalan Formations), South Pars Field, Iran


                                      CHAPTER 5


                           Diagenetic Controls on Porosity - Permeability



5.1   INTRODUCTION

In this chapter the effect that diagenetic processes have had on the sediments of the Kangan and
Dalan Formations in SP-9 are discussed, particularly how the pore systems have changed since
deposition and how these changes have influenced present day porosity-permeability. The major
diagenetic processes and products that have affected the depositional porosity in these wells include
carbonate dissolution (leaching), cementation, anhydrite formation, dolomitisation, compaction and
pressure solution, and the development of microporosity. A variety of fractures were seen in the core,
but the most common were microfractures. From observations of the core it appears that fracturing
may not significantly enhance the properties of the reservoir.

The diagenetic  studies described  in  this report not only involved core examination but also
conventional petrography to study open macroporosity and cement fill relationships. The results of
this study of the diagenesis are utilised in Chapter 6 of this report (Reservoir Facies and Reservoir
Characteristics) to explain the porosity-permeability trends of the various facies associations and
define reservoir facies.

This chapter  is subdivided into sections discussing the major processes in turn  i.e. Carbonate
Dissolution  (Section  5.2.1), Cementation  (5.2.2), Anhydrite Formation  (5.2.3), Dolomitisation
(5.2.4), Compaction and Pressure Solution  (5.2.5), Development of Microporosity (5.2.6) and
Fracturing (5.2.7). Minor processes are discussed in Section 5.2.8.

The effects of diagenesis are put into a sequence stratigraphic context in Section 5.3. The evolution
of the pore system is described for each sequence in an attempt to explain the vertical distribution of
porosity and permeability.

The distribution of the diagenetic minerals dolomite and anhydrite can be found in the lithology
column of the data summary charts for SP-9, which also contain wireline log profiles and measured
conventional core analysis porosity-permeability values displayed graphically (Enclosures 1-8). The
results of the brief petrographic analysis can be found in Appendix 1 and detailed analysis in
Appendix 2. Representative photomicrographs of diagenetic products and pore systems taken during
petrographic analysis are illustrated in Plates 1.1 to 3.15.

5.2   DIAGENETIC PROCESSES

Each of the diagenetic processes applicable to the Kangan and Dalan Formations of SP-9 are
described briefly below. Although models to explain these diagenetic processes are put forward they
do not constitute a comprehensive academic study of diagenesis. Such models are sufficiently

                                    Page 30 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 39 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


covered in the literature for example: Schreiber and El Tabakh, 2000; Purser et al., 1994; Tucker and
Wright, 1990; Longman, 1980; Bathurst, 1975; Reading 1978, 1996.

5.2.1  Carbonate Dissolution (Leaching)

The process of carbonate dissolution (or leaching) can enhance the reservoir quality of a formation
by forming secondary porosity, i.e. porosity formed after the sediment was deposited.

The process of leaching can produce secondary mouldic porosity by dissolution of component grains
and bioclasts, vugular porosity by modification of mouldic pores by dissolution of adjacent matrix
and solution channel pores by dissolution of matrix. In the Kangan and Dalan Formations mouldic
porosity is one of the most important secondary pore types and contributes a significant fraction of
the overall porosity. Most secondary porosity is formed by the dissolution of metastable grains such
as bioclasts and ooids composed of aragonite or low Mg calcite. Dissolution occurs when meteoric
rainwater enters the pore system during exposure (lowstand or late highstand systems tracts).

Mouldic porosity has developed in facies rich in ooids and oolitically coated grains, probably
originally composed of unstable aragonite and thus susceptible to leaching.  These grains are
common components of ooid and ooid intraclastic grainstone and packstone lithofacies (OGl, OGd,
OIGl, OIGd) and are also locally significant in the peloidal and peloidal intraclastic grainstone and
packstone lithofacies (PGl, PGd, IPGl, IPGd). Local vuggy and matrix dissolution macroporosity has
developed in the mudstone and wackestone lithofacies (MWl, MWd).

Carbonate dissolution or leaching of limestone sediments may also occur at a late stage in a burial
diagenetic environment.  It is theorised that leaching of sediment (both grains and matrix) can occur
associated with migration of hydrocarbons within the formation. Carbonic and/or organic acids can
be generated by CO2 present at the hydrocarbon front as migration occurs through the reservoir.
These acids will dissolve and leach grains and matrix, forming mouldic, vugular and intergranular
mud dissolution porosity.  Generation of porosity by this process is, however, speculative and may
account for only a few per cent porosity.

5.2.1.1 Karstic (vadose) dissolution

The process of leaching occurs in the vadose and freshwater phreatic zones of a sediment column
(Longman, 1980). The vadose diagenetic environment is the zone lying below the land surface and
above the water table. The freshwater phreatic zone lies below the water table, where all pore space
is filled with fresh water.  It is probable that most of the leaching observed in the Kangan and Dalan
Formations originated predominantly in the vadose environment.

Leaching occurs when pore waters are undersaturated with respect to calcium carbonate; meteoric
(rain) waters are a likely source of such fluids.  Meteoric water may have entered the sediment
column during sub-aerial exposure associated with the intertidal to supratidal sabkha sediments. In
SP-9 it is likely that meteoric water entered the pore system during late highstands associated with
sequence boundaries at, at least major sequence scale and probably 3rd order depositional sequence
scale.

5.2.2   Cementation

Carbonate cementation of the sediments occurred in two diagenetic environments: marine and
phreatic (burial). In SP-9, cementation by calcite, dolomite and anhydrite (discussed in Section 5.2.3)
has occurred.  Precipitation of calcite and dolomite are the most important cementation processes

                                    Page 31 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 40 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


affecting the pore systems.  Calcite occurs as early isopachous fringes, drusy and equant, and rare
later blocky calcite. Dolomite is commonly present replacing the calcite cements or as later cements.
Calcite and dolomite also occur as microcrystalline cementation associated with microporosity.

In the marine diagenetic environment isopachous fringes were initially precipitated, followed by
drusy and equant calcite cement. The marine diagenetic environment occurs at the sea floor where
primary  (depositional)  porosity  is  filled with  sea  water. Early marine cementation occurred
predominantly within the grainstone facies (e.g. OGl, BGl) as thin isopachous rim cements coating
grains (e.g. Plate 2.1). The cement is syn-depositional and the cement rims were probably initially
composed of aragonite or high Mg calcite and have subsequently been recrystallised (neomorphosed)
to drusy calcite (Longman, 1980). Similar cements have been recorded from 5 cm to 30 cm deep into
Recent intertidal sediments from the Arabian Gulf (Taylor and Illing, 1971), and a similar very
shallow marine, early diagenetic phase of cementation is considered likely for these facies. These
cements reduce porosity, but more importantly permeability, by restricting pore throats.

In the phreatic (burial) diagenetic environment blocky calcite cements were precipitated.  The
phreatic  burial diagenetic environment occurs  after burial below the influence of the marine
diagenetic environment,  all porosity being filled with water of variable saturations of calcium
carbonate. The cements were precipitated from meteoric (rain) waters, originating from the surface
that percolated down through the sediment column. As the meteoric water percolated downward it
became saturated in calcium carbonate by dissolution (leaching) of sediment grains and matrix.
When the waters become saturated in calcium carbonate, precipitation of blocky calcite will occur.
Calcium carbonate for the precipitation of cement can also be partially supplied by pressure solution
at stylolite boundaries in the burial diagenetic environment.

The rarely observed blocky calcite cement is predominantly non-ferroan and occurs as relatively
large, equant crystals. The cement has precipitated in small amounts in primary intergranular pores
between grains and in large mouldic pores in grainstones.

5.2.3   Anhydrite Formation

Anhydrite occurs as a major replacive phase, as a surface evaporitic phase and also as a cement phase
in SP-9. It forms significant thicknesses as laterally continuous layers of ‘chickenwire’ anhydrite and
chevron or nodular and laminar deposits, but it is also commonly present as isolated nodules and
isolated replacive crystals. The anhydrite is part of a well defined sabkha deposition and diagenetic
model which is well documented (Butler, 1969; Kinsman, 1969; Patterson and Kinsman, 1981).





                                    Page 32 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 41 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


has several distinctive textures, including palisade, where crystals can be seen growing from a
distinct surface, chevron, and nodular and laminar forms (Section 3.2.8).

Nodular replacive anhydrite in thin section has a ‘felted’ fabric comprising crystal laths or needles
(Plate 3.11), and also as poikilotopic crystals (Plates 3.8 and 3.10). Anhydrite nodules and cements
appear to be restricted to the dolomite lithologies.

5.2.4   Dolomitisation

Dolomitisation has variably replaced the precursor sediments in SP-9, from complete replacement to
dolomite rhombs scattered through the matrix.  Non-ferroan dolomite commonly occurs in SP-9,
replacing calcite sediments, and as a cement occluding mouldic, vugular, and intergranular pores
(Plates 3.1 and 3.9).  Saddle dolomite and ferroan dolomite are very rarely present, probably
suggesting a generally later, burial precipitation. A significant proportion of the cored interval is
unaffected (Enclosures 1 to 8, Table 6).

The dolomites in SP-9 are thought to form when dense, high Mg/Ca ratio, hypersaline brines sink
down and flow seaward through the intertidal and subtidal sediments by ‘seepage reflux’ resulting in
their replacement or cementation. A high density is achieved through the intense evaporation of
floodwaters which are driven on to the sabkha by tides or storms, and the high Mg/Ca ratio is
achieved by the precipitation of both gypsum and anhydrite. In addition to the seepage reflux
mechanism, another process termed ‘evaporative pumping’ may also have contributed  to the
dolomitisation (Mackenzie  et  al., 1980). Evaporative pumping refers to the landward flow of
seawater which replaces sabkha groundwater lost during evaporation. This process could provide a
continual flux of Mg ions for dolomitisation. For a good account of the various dolomite models see
Purser et al., 1994.

The petrographic characteristics and spatial distribution of the SP-9 dolomites suggest an origin
related to supratidal sabkha diagenesis with characteristics similar to those forming in the sabkhas of
the Persian Gulf at present.   Microcrystalline dolomite has replaced micrite matrix and grains of
micrite composition such as peloids (e.g. PGd, IPGd sub-lithofacies). This selective replacement
suggests that the precursor sediment controlled the crystal size of the dolomite replacement. Lime
muds can be preferentially dolomitised due to the presence of abundant nucleation sites in the very
fine grained sediment (Tucker and Wright, 1990).  This is commonly seen in modern supratidal
dolomites, and the total to partial dolomitisation of the MWd and MWl sub-lithofacies commonly
observed in SP-9, is suggestive of this origin.

Where the dolomite replacement has been more extensive, dolomite crystal fabrics have variably
interlocked non-planar  (xenotopic,  irregular  crystal  shapes), planar-S (hypplanar-e  (idiotopic),
subhedral crystals) and planar-e (planar-e (idiotopic), euhedral crystals) habits (Sibley and Gregg
1987, Friedman 1965) and very fine to medium crystalline dolomite has replaced bioclasts and non-
skeletal grains and occluded macroporosity in grain supported lithofacies (Plates 3.3 and 3.12). The
pervasive replacement by xenotopic to planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite commonly seen in the OGd and
OIGd sub-lithofacies probably occurred over a longer period of time, from shallow to deeper burial
and the retention of vague ghosts of ooids reflects an inability of the fluids to completely dissolve the
calcite/aragonite grains during early dolomitisation (Tucker and Wright, 1990).

5.2.5  Compaction and Pressure Solution

Mechanical compaction affects both mud supported and grain supported sediments.  In lime mud
dominated sediments, which often have depositional porosities of 50% to 70% (Enos and Sawatsky,

                                    Page 33 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 42 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


1981), compaction results in sediment dewatering and reorientation of platy grains.  In this way
significant matrix porosity is destroyed in the upper ten metres of burial. In grain supported sediment
compaction results in grain repacking until a rigid framework  is produced.   Early diagenetic
cementation or dolomitisation may also generate/enhance a  rigid framework and thus reduce
compaction and  porosity  loss. This has occurred  in  the grainstone and packstone/grainstone
dominated  sediments  of  the  ooid/ooid  intraclastic,  peloidal/peloidal  intraclastic,  bioclastic,
intraclastic and relict grain grainstones and packstones. Typically, early marine drusy cement formed
a  rigid framework before  burial and compaction of the sediment  in the carbonate cemented
sediments.  In the dolomites, the early cementation by dolomite or dolomitisation of the sediment
also formed a rigid framework to resist burial and compaction.

Pressure solution occurs when stress cannot be translated into mechanical compaction. This usually
occurs under deep burial conditions, after cementation or grain packing has produced a rigid
framework.  The initiation of pressure solution will, however, be dependent on a variety of other
factors, including the geothermal gradient, rock mineralogy and water chemistry.  The dominant
bedding parallel orientation of the pressure solution features shows they formed as a response to the
overlying sediment load.  In this way, pressure solution features are predominantly observed at bed
boundaries, often highlighting otherwise indistinct surfaces.

Pressure solution features in SP-9 consist predominantly of microstylolites and microsolution seams
with local stylolites, solution seams and rare solution seam swarms (Enclosures 1-8). Solution seams
are smooth, undulating solution surfaces, whereas  stylolites and microstylolites are  digitating
solution  surfaces  (Wanless,  1979). The  pressure  solution  features  are  generally  present  as
microsolution features (less than 1 cm amplitude), but are also locally seen as relatively poorly
developed stylolites/solution seams with amplitudes of generally less than 2 cm and are most
commonly present as microsolution features (less than 1 cm amplitude). They occur in the clean
limestone lithologies, commonly at surfaces, but are more common in the rare, more argillaceous
limestones.

5.2.6  Development of Microporosity

Within the Kangan and Dalan Formations of South Pars-9, microporosity (pores <20 µm in size) is
locally significant within the reservoir sediments.  It occurs within the micrite matrix of mudstones,
wackestones and packstones and also within micritic grains such as pellets and peloids in packstones
and grainstones. Within dolomitised sediments, particularly the MWd and PGd sub-lithofacies,
intercrystalline microporosity can be very significant, particularly in a gas reservoir. In thin section
it is recognised by blue dye impregnation of the microporosity, resulting in a very dark greenish
colour to matrix and grains (e.g. Plate 2.5). The petrophysical characteristics of a lime micropore
system are relatively high porosity but low permeability resulting from the small pore and pore throat
size, however, within a microcrystalline to crystalline dolomitised sediment the characteristics can be
reversed resulting in a low porosity, but a moderate to good permeability which can be easily
exploited by gas. Microporosity also locally occurs where the matrix has undergone dissolution and
generally results in the largest micropores.

The preservation of microporosity in mudstone and wackestone sediments is thought to occur when a
compaction  resistant framework formed  in the micrite matrix or micrite grains due  to early
cementation at the contact points of micrite crystals. Intercrystalline microporosity is then preserved
because of this rigid crystal framework.  It is thought that the microporosity is a product of a two-step
diagenetic process.   Firstly, mineralogical stabilisation of lime mud (aragonite/high Mg calcite)
occurred,  then  calcite-calcite  recrystallisation  produced  the  micrite  crystal  framework  with
intercrystalline microporosity.  Cementation of the micrite crystals occurred at crystal contacts but

                                    Page 34 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 43 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


ceased shortly after stabilisation was complete.  This produced a compaction resistant fabric,
composed of stable low Mg calcite, which remained uncemented because there was no introduction
of allochthonous calcite to  fill porosity, as fluid flow of calcite saturated waters through the
micropore system was low because of the low permeability resulting from small pore throats. Within
packstone sediments this mechanism also occurs, but the matrix microporosity may also be preserved
by the formation of a rigid framework of the macrograins with the intergranular matrix remaining
uncompacted.

5.2.7   Fracturing

The dominant form of fracturing observed in SP-9 is microfractures which occur throughout the well,
but are predominantly associated with the mudstones and wackestones, and are particularly common
in association with stylolites and solution seams.  These microfractures can provide a significant
local  permeability conduit  in  otherwise impermeable  sediments,  particularly those  that have
undergone relatively strong cementation prior to the fracture formation.  Few open fractures are
present in SP-9; however, there are abundant strongly anhydrite and dolomite cemented fractures
which will act as permeability barriers rather than conduits.

5.2.8  Minor Diagenetic Minerals

Pyrite is present in minor amounts in the SP-9 core and was present in trace amounts in many of the
petrographically analysed samples. The pyrite is typically scattered throughout the matrix, but may
also be intimately associated with cements. Where associated with cementation, the pyrite appears to
form after early marine cementation, but prior to the deposition of coarse, blocky burial cements.
This is consistent with the formation of pyrite within the sulphate reduction zone, with the presence
of organic matter during burial of the sediment.

5.3   THE EFFECTS OF DIAGENESIS ON THE STRATIGRAPHIC SEQUENCES

The diagenesis affecting well SP-9 is a very complex interplay of reservoir enhancing and reducing
processes, which are not generally specific to the individual sequences or lithofacies (Figure 3).
However, some generalisations can be made and are addressed below.

5.3.1  KS4 Major Sequence

The lower part of this sequence (KS4a) is a strongly dolomitised and variably anhydrite cemented
section with generally low porosity but variable permeability.  The presence of strong anhydrite
cementation  generally  reduces  porosity  and  permeability  significantly,  however,  where  the
dolomitised sediment  is not anhydrite cemented there may be locally significant retention of
permeability (Figures 4 and 5).

The upper section of the KS4 major sequence (KS4b) is variably composed of dolomitised and
calcitic sediments. The change in lithology is generally coincident with described surfaces in the
core, however it should be noted that these surfaces do not usually reflect a lithofacies or facies
association boundary.  Within this interval very good reservoir facies have been developed in the
dolomitic sediments by the generation of high intercrystalline porosity, which is extremely well
connected, during the replacement of oolitic and peloidal grains. In comparison with other wells (i.e.
SP-5) dolomite appears to be volumetrically more important in the lower part of the KS4b-3
depositional unit. The reason for this is unclear but it may be that in SP-9, KS4b-3 was the location
of a zone of mixing which may laterally continue east. If this is correct, the dolomite lithology will

                                    Page 35 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 44 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


also be present in that direction until the limits of the mixing zone are reached and the pore system is
filled with sea water with no meteoric influence.

Within the calcitic units the good reservoir quality reflects the presence of extremely large amounts
of oomouldic porosity generated by the dissolution of aragonitic ooids. The permeability connected
with these sediments is lower due to the presence of pre-existing early marine intergranular cements.
It is probable that the undersaturated meteoric water required to dissolve the ooids entered the
primary intergranular pore system during emergence at the end of KS4 deposition during late
highstand.

A thin, medium to coarse grained oomoldic and biomouldic interval within this sequence (3174.75-
3177.25m LD) probably provides some of the best flow in the reservoir.

5.3.2  KS3 Major Sequence

The KS3 sequence has been almost entirely dolomitised, with the exception of a 10m interval
towards the base of the sequence which is predominantly calcitic, but locally dolomitic. The calcitic
unit at the base of the interval predominantly comprises mudstones with little or no reservoir quality.
Dolomitisation of the upper part of the sequence has resulted in all of the facies associations
retaining very little porosity, however some permeability does remain within low porosity grainstone
shoals and foreshoal mudstones. The only good reservoir sediments present in this sequence lie in
the thin dolomitised foreshoal and burrowed shoal intervals at the base of the sequence (Figures 6
and 7).

5.3.3  KS2 Major Sequence

KS2 can be broadly separated into three diagenetic intervals. At the base, the KS2-1 and 2 cycles are
predominantly  dolomitised,  but do provide  locally good  reservoir  facies,  particularly  in  the
dolomitised mudstones and ooid grainstones. The middle part of KS2 (KS2-3) is entirely calcitic and
depositionally is predominantly medium grained oolitic shoal sediments.  This section of SP-9
provides one of the best reservoir sections with good oomouldic porosity formed during the leaching
of aragonitic ooids (e.g. Plates 3.5 and 3.6). The uppermost section of KS2 (KS2-4 and 5) is strongly
dolomitised; however, the pervasive dolomitisation of the ooid shoal has evolved extremely good
intercrystalline and intergranular porosity and permeability in KS2-4 (Figure 8).

5.3.4  KS1 Major Sequence

The KS1 sequence is generally dolomitic; however, there are calcitic shoals present within cycles
KS1b-1 and KS1a-3, which locally provide moderately good reservoir intervals (Figure 9).  The
distribution of the dolomitic and calcitic intervals does not appear to reflect obvious depositional
factors, although changes in diagenesis do commonly, but not exclusively, occur at lithofacies
boundaries.



                                    Page 36 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 45 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


                                                    Reservoir Geological Study of Cores from the
                            SP-9 Well (Kangan and Dalan Formations), South Pars Field, Iran


                                      CHAPTER 6


                          Reservoir Facies and Reservoir Characteristics



6.1   INTRODUCTION

The identification of reservoir facies within SP-9 is dependent upon the petrophysical characteristics
of the sediments.  The aim of characterising reservoir facies is to identify petrophysically similar
rock units, which are a function of their original depositional lithofacies and later diagenetic
overprint. However, it is notable that the reservoir facies defined in SP-9 are commonly, although
not exclusively described by their depositional and diagenetic history.  Thus, although the best
reservoir facies are typically those deposited as shoal sediments, dolomitised or leached. Sediments
from other depositional facies associations e.g. foreshoal or lagoon, may also be present in these
reservoir facies having similar petrophysical characteristics. Petrophysically the boundaries between
the reservoir facies locally overlap and could be viewed as somewhat artificial; however, they do
generally result in meaningful divisions. Fifteen reservoir facies were recognised grading from RF1
having the best reservoir quality to RF15 with the poorest.

The basis for the reservoir facies characterisation is given in Section 6.2.1 and each reservoir facies
is described in Section 6.2.2 (illustrated by Plates 3.1-3.15 and reservoir facies montages Figures 10-
13).  In Section 6.3 the reservoir facies are summarised in terms of their sequence stratigraphic
distribution (See also Figures 3-9) and the reservoir characteristics are summarised in Table 11.

6.2   RESERVOIR FACIES SCHEME

6.2.1   Reservoir Facies Characterisation

The reservoir facies were characterised using all of the available information regarding deposition,
diagenesis and sequence stratigraphy.   Initially, the data were ascribed to lithofacies and sub-
lithofacies (Section 3) based upon the detailed core description and thin section analysis. Analysis of
data from core description and thin section analysis resulted in the identification of specific trends
which reflect certain petrophysical characteristics on the porosity-permeability cross-plots of the
lithofacies and sub-lithofacies (Figures 10-13).  It was these petrophysical characteristics that were
used to delineate groups of data into the reservoir facies described below (Section 6.2.2).

The three lithofacies which contained the most data points with the largest numbers of thin sections,
allowing the most detailed analysis were the oolitic grainstones and packstones, peloidal grainstones
and packstones, and the mudstones and wackestones.

Analysis of the data from the oolitic and oolitic intraclastic grainstones (OGl/OGd/OIGl/OIGd sub-
lithofacies)  (Figures 10-13, oolitic and oolitic intraclastic grainstone lithofacies cross plots) shows
that there is considerable variation in reservoir quality.  It can be seen that there are two major sub-
groups of data, namely the dolomitised (OGd) and lime oolites (OGl), which each further comprise
                                    Page 37 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 46 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


two, slightly overlapping sub-groups. The oolitic lime grainstones generally fall into two groups,
which are defined by variations in grain size and diagenesis. The oolitic lime grainstones with well
developed mouldic pores (porosity generally >15%) have very good porosity, but it is dominated by
mouldic pores which are poorly connected, due to the presence of pervasive intergranular calcite
cement (Plates 3.5 and 3.6). As such, the permeability generally ranges from only 0.1 mD to 50 mD.
The variation in reservoir quality is controlled here by the variation in grain size and presence of
small amounts of dolomite cement (RF 6, 7 and 9). The lower porosity, higher permeability oolitic
lime grainstones (OGl sub-lithofacies) are typically medium to coarse grained with oomouldic pores
with small amounts of dolomite cement present (RF7 and 5) (Plate 3.5). However, as the grain size of
the oomoulds and amount  of  dolomite cement  reduces,  the  porosity  rapidly  increases and
permeability slightly decreases (smaller pore throats, Plate 3.6, RF6). The poorer quality oolitic lime
grainstones are characterised by the presence of dolomitised ooids and abundant early marine calcite
cement (RF9 and 12, also seen in the higher porosity oolitic lime grainstone group) (Plates 3.9 and
3.12).

The best reservoir potential in the grainstone lithofacies is in the dolomitised planar-e (idiotopic)
grainstones  with  abundant  intercrystalline  and  relict  intergranular  porosity  and  very  high
permeability (up to 3800 mD, RF1 to 4) (Plates 3.1-3.4). The poorer reservoir quality OGd sub-
lithofacies are characterised by the presence of variable amounts of anhydrite cement, which
increases with decreasing porosity and permeability (RF 8, 10, 13) (Plates 3.8, 3.10, 3.13).

The peloidal grainstones and packstones comprise three main trends of data, controlled by grain size
and diagenesis, resulting in significant variations in their reservoir facies and characteristics. The
best reservoir facies grouping of the PGl and PGd lithofacies are the dolomitised grainstones with a
planar-e  (idiotopic)  fabric, which has retained good  intercrystalline porosity and permeability
(Figures 11  to  13).   The  very  fine  grained  peloidal  lime  packstones  exhibit good  micro-
intercrystalline porosity where the grains have not been dolomitised (RF5 and 7), however, where the
grains are dolomitised and there is strong calcite cementation the reservoir quality is significantly
reduced (RF12 and 15) (Plates 3.12, 3.15). The trend for calcite cemented, dolomitised grains is the
same trend that was seen in the oolitic lime grainstones and was used to delineate Reservoir Facies
15 and 12. The anhydrite cemented PGd sediments also show a distinct trend, similar to that seen in
the anhydrite cemented OGd lithofacies plots, and diagenetic grouping was used to define Reservoir
Facies 13, 10 and 8.

The mudstones and wackestones comprise four main data groups again controlled by grain size and
diagenesis (Figure 13).  The best reservoir quality  is seen where macro-intercrystalline pores
dominate the dolomitised mudstones and wackestones (MWd), resulting from the generation of a
planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite texture (RF2 and 5) (Plate 3.2). The reservoir quality generally reduces
as the intercrystalline pore size reduces (RF7 and 10) (Plate 3.7). Again the same trend as in the PGl
and OGl lithofacies is seen for partially dolomitised and calcite cemented mudstones (RF 15 and 12),
however the trend of low porosity and rapidly increasing permeability ascribed to the variable
presence of anhydrite is here the result of the dolomitic fabric becoming increasingly more open,
from xenotopic to hypplanar-e (idiotopic)/planar-e (idiotopic). Where good permeability is present it
is largely due to the presence of microfractures, which are locally abundant in the mudstones and
wackestones (RF11 and 14) (Plate 3.14).

This detailed analysis of the trends and grouping of reservoir characteristics was also carried out on
the remaining lithofacies and used, with the facies association plots, to define the following reservoir
facies (Figure 3).

[ صفحه 47 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────

6.2.2   Description of Reservoir Facies

6.2.2.1 Reservoir facies RF1

Average Porosity = 26.6%           Permeability = 1992 mD

Reservoir facies 1 (RF1) has the best reservoir quality (Porosity: >22%, Permeability: >150 mD).
RF1 generally comprises fine to very finely crystalline, planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite replacing
barrier shoal ooid grainstones.  Relict intergranular and intercrystalline porosity is extremely well
developed and connectivity is excellent (Plate 3.1). RF1 occurs predominantly within sediments of
the barrier shoal complex facies association; the favourable reservoir characteristics are illustrated on
the cross plot with porosity-permeability being consistently high (Figure 11).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF1 is likely to be extensive being part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity. This is supported by consideration of wireline logs from
adjacent wells, although the geometry may be restricted by the dolomitisation process. There is some
indication that dolomitisation increases eastwards.

6.2.2.2 Reservoir facies RF2

Average Porosity = 30.0%                    Permeability = 63 mD

Reservoir facies 2 (RF2) also has good reservoir quality (Porosity: 27%-33.5%, Permeability: 13-
151 mD) and is dominated by extremely finely crystalline dolomitised mudstones. These mudstones
contain extremely fine, intercrystalline macroporosity with planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite fabric
resulting in very good connectivity (Plate  3.2). These favourable reservoir  characteristics are
illustrated on the cross plot with consistently high porosity and moderate to high permeability (Figure
3). RF2 occurs within sediments of the foreshoal, burrowed shoal and restricted lagoon facies
associations (Figures 10, 11 and 12).

Geometry: The geometry is restricted by the dolomitisation process which appears to have occurred
at specific horizons that are laterally continuous over the  field. There  is some indication that
dolomitisation increases eastwards.

6.2.2.3 Reservoir facies RF3

Average Porosity = 17.4%           Permeability = 558 mD

Reservoir facies 3 (RF3) exhibits very good reservoir quality (Porosity: 12%-21%, Permeability:
132-2083 mD). RF3 almost exclusively comprises medium grained, planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite
replacing ooid and bioclastic grainstones. Relict intergranular porosity is well developed although
partial  filling by dolomite  is apparent. The excellent connectivity of the pore system  is via
intercrystalline and relict intergranular pore throats connecting these pores resulting in the high
permeability values (Plate 3.3). RF3 occurs predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal
complex facies association (Figure 11).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF3 is likely to be extensive being part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity. This is supported by consideration of wireline logs from
adjacent wells although the geometry may be restricted by the dolomitisation process.

                                    Page 39 r

[ صفحه 48 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


6.2.2.4 Reservoir facies RF4

Average Porosity = 18.9%                    Permeability = 50 mD

Reservoir facies 4 (RF4) has moderate to good reservoir quality (Porosity: 13%-27%, Permeability:
11-178 mD). RF4 is generally composed of dolomitised fine to medium grained peloidal and oolitic
packstones and grainstones (Plate 3.4) or very finely crystalline planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite. Relict
intergranular porosity is interconnected via intercrystalline microporosity (<20 µm pore size) which
controls reservoir quality. The small intercrystal pore throats result in the 10-200 mD permeability
values. RF4 occurs predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal complex and lagoon facies
associations (Figures 11 and 12).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF4 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex. Where RF4 is part of the lagoonal
association (shoal spillovers) then a limited distribution is expected.  As with other dolomitised
sediments, the geometry may be restricted by the dolomitisation process.

6.2.2.5 Reservoir facies RF5

Average Porosity = 21.9%         Permeability = 18 mD

Reservoir facies 5 (RF5) has moderate to good reservoir quality (Porosity: 16%-27%, Permeability:
1-100 mD) and the rock predominantly consists of oolitic, calcite cemented grainstones (Plate 3.5)
and fine grained dolomitised peloidal grainstones.  Extensive leaching of ooids has produced a
characteristic fabric of abundant moudic pores isolated by intergranular calcite cement rimming the
pores. Therefore, although porosity is good to very good, connectivity is poor resulting in the
generally low permeability. Generally, the higher permeability sediments in RF4 comprise the
dolomitised mudstones. RF5 occurs predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal complex and
the foreshoal facies association as shoal washovers (Figures 11 and 10).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF5 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association. Where RF5 is
part of the foreshoal facies association, as shoal washovers, then a limited distribution is expected.
As with other dolomitised sediments, the geometry may be restricted by the dolomitisation process.

6.2.2.6 Reservoir facies RF6

Average Porosity = 29.5%                    Permeability = 1.7 mD

Reservoir facies 6 (RF6) has moderate reservoir quality (Porosity: 24%-47%, Permeability: 0.07-
7.3 mD) and is composed of fine to very fine grained calcite cemented, oolitic grainstones (OGl,
OIGl  sub-lithofacies,  Plate  3.6). As with RF5,  extensive leaching of ooids has produced a
characteristic fabric of abundant mouldic pores isolated by intergranular calcite cement rimming the
pores. Therefore although porosity is good, connectivity is poor resulting in the generally low
permeability. There appears to be a tenuous relationship between grain size and permeability. RF6
occurs within sediments of the barrier shoal complex (Figure 11).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF6 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association.

6.2.2.7 Reservoir facies RF7

Average Porosity = 13.9%                    Permeability = 6.1mD

Reservoir facies RF7 has moderate to poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 1.84%-18.7%, Permeability:
0.01-27.4 mD) and  is composed  of a number  of  sub-lithofacies and  diagenetic  associations.
Sediments falling within RF7 include dolomitised mudstones (MWd, Plate 3.7) with moderate
intercrystalline microporosity, very fine grained dolomitised peloidal packstones with intercrystalline
microporosity and ooid grainstones with varying amounts of mouldic porosity and dolomitised
grains.  The dolomitised mudstones and peloidal packstones are dominated by intercrystalline
microporosity (<20 µm pore size) resulting in the relatively low permeability. The ooid grainstones
have abundant mouldic pores but are interconnected via a matrix micropore system also with small
pore throat, which also results in the low permeability. RF7 occurs predominantly within sediments
of the barrier shoal complex and lagoon facies associations (Figures 11 and 12).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF7 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association.

6.2.2.8 Reservoir facies RF8

Average Porosity = 10.6%                    Permeability = 112 mD

Reservoir facies 8 (RF8) has a generally low porosity (7%-13%), but can be considered to have
moderate reservoir quality due to its good permeability (11-532 mD). RF8 is dominated by medium
grained dolomitised ooid grainstones with low to moderate amounts of anhydrite cementation (Plate
3.8).  Planar-e (idiotopic) dolomitisation and only patchy anhydrite cement has resulted in the
preservation of good relict intergranular pores with moderate to poor connectivity. RF8 occurs
predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal complex facies associations (Figure 11).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF8 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association.

6.2.2.9 Reservoir facies RF9

Average Porosity = 18.4%                    Permeability = 0.2 mD

Reservoir facies 9 (RF9) has poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 15%-22%, Permeability: 0.05-0.5 mD).
RF9 is predominantly composed of medium to fine grained oolitic grainstones with intergranular
pores occluded by early marine calcite cements and oomouldic pores partially to totally occluded by
planar-e  (euhedral) dolomite rhombs  (Plate  3.9). These  effects of cementation have reduced
connectivity of the pore system resulting in the relatively low permeability. RF9 also includes
dolomitised mudstones with a micro-intercrystalline pore system. Fine intercrystalline pore throats
result in low permeability. RF9 occurs in all of the facies associations with the oolitic grainstones
particularly well developed in barrier shoal complex (Figure 11). The dolomitised mudstones occur
within the restricted lagoon facies association (Figure 12).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF9 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association and the
restricted lagoon facies association although the latter will be limited by the extent of dolomitisation.
As the lagoons are juxtaposed with tidal flat environments, a diagenetic site of dolomitisation,
dolomitisation is probably extensive.

[ صفحه 50 از 133 ]

6.2.2.10 Reservoir facies RF10

Average Porosity = 6.9%                     Permeability = 14 mD

Reservoir facies 10 (RF10) has poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 3%-11%, Permeability: 0.8-70 mD),
although moderate permeability is locally retained (Figure 3). RF10 consists of medium grained,
dolomitised ooid and peloidal grainstones (OGd, PGd sub-lithofacies, Plate 3.10) which have been
more strongly anhydrite cemented than those of RF8 (Plate 3.8). This occlusion of large proportions
of the intergranular and relict intergranular pore systems has greatly reduced the effective porosity of
these grainstone facies. Where the permeability is highest, the sample may be locally fractured.
RF10 occurs predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal complex although it also occurs in
lagoon and foreshoal environments as shoal spillovers and washovers (Figure 11).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF10 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the  barrier shoal complex  facies association. When
associated with other facies associations, distribution will be limited.

6.2.2.11 Reservoir facies RF11

Average Porosity = 6.3%                     Permeability = 276 mD

Reservoir facies 11 (RF11) has moderate to good reservoir quality because of the high permeability
locally retained in these dolomitised sediments (Porosity: 3%-10%, Permeability: 53-1524 mD). The
permeability is generated through open micro-fractures, which is reflected in the wide range of sub-
lithofacies present in this reservoir facies (OIGd, IPGd, BGd, RGd, OGd, MWd) (Plate 3.11). The
majority of primary macroporosity has been occluded by anhydrite cement. Patchy porosity and good
permeability may also occur within burrows.

Geometry: Although present in all facies association, the distribution of RF11 cannot be determined
as its petrophysical character is controlled by fracturing and diagenesis not facies.

6.2.2.12 Reservoir facies RF12

Average Porosity = 10.5%                    Permeability = 0.5 mD

Reservoir facies 12 (RF12), with very poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 7%-13%, Permeability: 0.05-
3.5 mD), is dominated by tight ooid and peloidal grainstones.  The OGl and PGl sub-lithofacies
grainstones have dolomitised grains and have been strongly calcite cemented resulting in significant
reduction  of  the  reservoir  quality  (Plate  3.12). As a consequence  of  this cementation and
replacement, primary intergranular and secondary mouldic porosity has been greatly reduced. RF12
occurs predominantly within sediments of the barrier shoal complex.

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF12 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association

6.2.2.13 Reservoir facies RF13

Average Porosity = 1.7%                     Permeability = 0.8 mD

Reservoir facies 13 (RF13) has very poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 0.01%-8.5%, Permeability:
0.01-10 mD). RF13 contains extensively anhydrite cemented and replaced grainstone and packstone
sediments from the majority of sub-lithofacies (Plate 3.13).  Where permeability is highest, local
porosity has been retained in oolitic grainstones, but generally most or all macroporosity has been
occluded by anhydrite in this reservoir facies.

Geometry: This reservoir facies is characterised by diagenetic overprint as opposed to facies and
depositional  texture. As  anhydrite cementation/replacemen, RF13  will occur juxtaposed with
anhydrite layers as this will be the primary source of the cement.

6.2.2.14 Reservoir facies RF14

Average Porosity = 1.6%                     Permeability = 37 mD

Reservoir facies 14 (RF14) has poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 0.2%-4%, Permeability: 6-126 mD).
Any permeability remaining in these sediments is the result of microfracturing of the predominantly
mudstone and wackestone lithofacies. Much of the microfracturing may be artefact, although some
does appear to be real (Plate 3.14).

Geometry: Although present in all facies associations the distribution of RF11 cannot be determined
as its petrophysical character is controlled by diagenesis not facies.

6.2.2.15 Reservoir facies RF15

Average Porosity = 3.2% Permeability = 0.1 mD

Reservoir facies 15 has extremely poor reservoir quality (Porosity: 0.01%-9%, Permeability: 0.01-
2 mD) because of the extensive effects of diagenetic overprinting of original depositional texture. As
diagenesis is the reservoir quality controlling factor, a variety of lithofacies are included in this
category. A large proportion of RF15 comprises oolitic and peloidal grainstones of the barrier shoal
facies association. These sediments have been extensively cemented by calcite or dolomite and a
large component of grains have been replaced by non-planar (xenotopic) dolomite. In addition,
intergranular and relict intergranular porosity is cemented by early marine calcite (Plate 3.15). The
detrimental affect on porosity-permeability of this cementation and replacement are graphically
illustrated in Figure 11. Matrix supported sediments also occur in this category from foreshoal,
lagoon and tidal flat facies associations. These predominantly mudstone and wackestone lithofacies
are commonly partially dolomitised with a completely neomorphically recrystallised calcite matrix.
As a consequence of this diagenetic recrystallisation any microporosity or mouldic porosity has all
but been destroyed (Figures 10, 12 and 13).

Geometry: Lateral continuity of RF15 is likely to be extensive when part of a prograding/aggrading
system of high sediment productivity of the barrier shoal complex facies association.

6.3   SUMMARY OF RESERVOIR FACIES DISTRIBUTION

In this section, the vertical distribution of the reservoir facies is discussed in both the context of the
sequence stratigraphic framework and also the established reservoir-layering scheme (Table 12).

                                    Page 43 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 52 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Most of the reservoir facies intervals are relatively thin which is an indication of the heterogeneity of
the present pore system of the sediments.

6.3.1  KS4 Major Sequence

6.3.1.1 KS4a depositional sequence

Sequence KS4a is dominated by poor quality reservoir facies, predominantly RF10, 11, 13, 14 and 15
(Figure  4).   These  reservoir  facies are  typically moderately  to strongly anhydrite cemented,
dolomitised tidal flat or barrier shoal sediments. At the top of the sequence there is a thin (1m-2m)
laminae of moderate reservoir quality facies, RF4 and 5; however, this is likely to be discontinuous
and have poor connectivity to the main reservoir intervals above. This depositional sequence occurs
immediately above the Nar Formation and comprises reservoir layers 16 and 17.

6.3.1.2 KS4b depositional sequence

Sequence KS4b  is predominantly composed of variably dolomitised barrier shoal and lagoon
sediments (Figure 5). The interval is dominated by good to excellent reservoir facies (RF1-7) with
only minor units of poor reservoir quality which predominantly occur in lower parts of the sequence
(KS4b-1 and -2). However, thin poor reservoir quality streaks do occur throughout this sequence and
are generally correlatable with the presence of foreshoal (FS) and tidal flat (TF) mudstones. The
good to excellent reservoir facies seen in this sequence are from the shoal interior and shoreface
facies associations and the shoal washovers into the lagoon, and are typically composed of fine to
medium grained oolitic shoals. Within the oolitic grainstones the best reservoir quality has evolved
where the grainstones have been strongly dolomitised and porosity is predominantly well connected
intercrystalline and intergranular pore types.  However, very high porosity values (up to 47%) are
recorded in the oolitic grainstones (OGl sub-lithofacies) (RF 5, 6 and 7) where the porosity is
dominated by oomouldic pores but the presence of early marine calcite cement has removed much of
the permeability.

Within  this sequence the interval from 3174.5-3177.5m LD had been previously identified as
providing about 60% of flow of gas from this reservoir. This interval is dominated by oolitic barrier
shoal grainstones, which are locally dolomitised, and of extremely good reservoir quality (RF1, 3, 4
and 5).  This interval is distinguished by its massive nature and the presence of more common
mouldic, relict bioclasts than are seen in other oolitic shoal sediments.  It appears that this mixture of
oolitic grains and leached bioclasts provides the optimum reservoir interval for this well (SP-9). The
presence of calcite or dolomite in this interval does not appear to significantly affect the reservoir
quality. The KS4b depositional sequence contains reservoir layers 10 to 16.

6.3.2  KS3 Major Sequence

6.3.2.1 KS3a depositional sequence

Sequence KS3a is dominated by strongly anhydrite cemented, poor quality reservoir facies (RF13,
15) which, where locally fractured, are a slightly better quality reservoir facies (RF14) (Figure 6).
Within these generally tight sediments there are thin laminae of relatively good quality reservoir
facies (RF 2, 5 and 7), which are predominantly the result of grainy flows of shoal material into the
deeper middle shelf foreshoal (FSa). These relatively good reservoir facies only occur in significant
thicknesses at the base of the KS3a-2 and 3 cycles.  These sediments are typically dolomitised
mudstones and wackestones (MWd sub-lithofacies) or dolomitised peloidal packstones (PGd) with
well developed, fine grained intercrystalline porosity preserved in the planar-e (idiotopic) dolomite

                                    Page 44 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 53 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


fabric (MWd) and micro-intercrystalline porosity developed within dolomitised grains and matrix
(PGd).  The poor quality reservoir facies comprising the majority of this sequence reflects the
presence of relatively strong anhydrite cementation and the abundance of intertidal sediments which
are dominated by thinly interbedded, low porosity mudstones and wackestones (MWl, MWd). The
KS3a depositional sequence contains the lower part of reservoir layer 8 (KS3a-7 to KS3a4ii) and
layer 9.

6.3.2.2 KS3b depositional sequence

Sequence KS3b is also dominated by relatively poor quality reservoir facies (RF13, 15, 10 and 11)
(Figure  7),  reflecting the  relative dominance of the generally poorer  reservoir  quality facies
associations  (foreshoal, lagoon and  intertidal  flats) and  the  significant amount  of  anhydrite
cementation, which has affected the packstone and grainstone units.  This entire sequence  is
dolomitised, but  is dominated by xenotopic dolomite textures, which also significantly reduce
porosity and permeability.  The KS3b depositional sequence contains the top of reservoir layer 8
(KS3b-4 to -1) and the lower part of layer 7.

6.3.3  KS2 Major Sequence

Sequence KS2 is dominated by barrier shoal sediments (BS) with a wide range of reservoir quality
(Figure 8). The best reservoir quality sediments (RF1-5) in KS2 are developed in KS2-2 unit and
comprise a shallowing upwards barrier shoal cycle composed of both calcite and dolomitised oolitic
grainstones (OGl, OGd sub-lithofacies), relict grain grainstones (RGd) and dolomitised mudstones
and wackestones (MWd). The variation in grain size, texture and diagenesis within this cycle has
resulted in the wide range of reservoir facies observed.  The remainder of the KS2 sequence is
generally composed of relatively poor quality reservoir facies reflecting the abundance of intertidal
flat and lagoon facies association sediments observed outside of the KS2-2 cycle. The KS2 Major
Sequence contains reservoir layers 4 to the upper part of layer 7 (KS2-1 to -3).

6.3.4  KS1 Major Sequence

Sequence KS1 is dominated by the predominantly low to no reservoir quality reservoir facies of
RF13 and RF15 (Figure 9). Locally, there are units of good reservoir quality present (predominantly
RF 4, 6 and 8) which are a response to the presence of thin units of calcitic and dolomitised oolitic
grainstone (OGl/OGd) barrier shoal (BS) sediments. These relatively good quality reservoir facies
are of variable thickness but are separated by thick, laterally continuous layers of the dominant non-
reservoir units and are probably not vertically connected to the main reservoir units in the well. The
KS1b Major Sequence contains reservoir layers 1 to 3.

[ صفحه 86 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                       Report No.8525 /Id


PLATE 2.1

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Core Depth:                 3239.05 mDD        Measured Porosity:        5.6%

Sequence:                 KS4a-3               Horizontal Permeability:     1.9mD

Reservoir Layer:          R16                  Lithofacies:            OGl/OGd

Facies Association:         Oolitic grainstone and packstone

A general view of a dolomitised ooid grainstone (OGd), with common micritised and dolomitised
peloids (F7, H2). The ooids are replaced by fabric retentive dolomite. Numerous ooids still retain a
trace of the original concentric structure  (e.g. E4). Early marine aragonite or high-Mg calcite
isopachous cements have also been dolomitised (e.g. F4, C7), and along with patchy anhydrite cement
(e.g. C7), have occluded much of the intergranular porosity. Local intergranular (G9) and mouldic
porosity (e.g. B9) is visible, but poorly connected.





PLATE 2.2

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Core Depth:                 2984.96 mDD        Measured Porosity:      NMP

Sequence:                 KS1a-2               Horizontal Permeability:      1.1 mD

Reservoir Layer:          R3                   Lithofacies:             PGl/PGd

Facies Association:         Peloidal grainstone and packstone

A  general view  of a moderately well  sorted,  dolomitised  peloidal grainstone (PGd), showing
recrystallised peloids  (e.g. B8), micro-peloids (D8), and dolomitic microspar, resulting from the
recrystallisation of matrix. Intergranular pores are partially occluded by dolomite microspar, and
common anhydrite cement (I4). Intergranular macroporosity (E1) and microporosity (F10) are locally
preserved, but poorly connected.

PLATE 2.3

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Core Depth:                 2894.03 mDD        Measured Porosity:        0.5%

Sequence:                 KS1a-3               Horizontal Permeability:      0.01 mD

Reservoir Layer:          R2                   Lithofacies:            BGl

Facies Association:         Bioclastic grainstone and packstone

A general view of a tight, bioclastic packstone. Bioclastic grains include gastropods (A7, A4-C1),
bivalves (D4, C11), ostracods (G9, A8) and indeterminate skeletal grains (H11, B6). Non-skeletal
grains include micritised, micro-peloids (E9). The non-skeletal grains have been strongly micritised.
The skeletal grains, which were predominantly aragonitic in composition have been leached and
replaced by drusy and micromosaic non-ferroan calcite (e.g. C7, H10) and rare dolomite (E5). No
visible porosity is observed.





PLATE 2.4

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Core Depth:                 3178.80 mDD        Measured Porosity:         25.1%

Sequence:                KS4b-3               Horizontal Permeability:     14834 mD

Reservoir Layer:          R14                  Lithofacies:          RGd

Facies Association:         Relict grain grainstone and packstone

A general view of a dolomitised relict grain grainstone. Vague ghosts of the original depositional
texture of this rock are preserved (e.g. F6, G3). The replacement dolomite has an open idiotopic
texture, resulting in good intercrystalline porosity (F9-I5) and very well connected permeability.

PLATE 2.5

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Core Depth:                 3203.04 mDD        Measured Porosity:        1.1%

Sequence:                KS4b-1               Horizontal Permeability:    NMP

Reservoir Layer:          R15                  Lithofacies:        MWd

Facies Association:       Mudstone and Wackestone

A general view of a dolomitised mudstone (MWd). The sample is generally devoid of grains, however,
scattered skeletal debris (e.g. F9, G7) and dark coloured peloids are rarely observed (G2 and C1). The
recrystallisation of the originally calcitic or aragonitic mudstone, in addition to the occurrence of rare
pyrite (B6), has occluded much of the microporosity.





PLATE 2.6

Well:                      SP-9                  Scale Bar:               500µm

Sequence:                KS4b-5               Horizontal Permeability:     33 mD

Reservoir Layer:          R10                  Lithofacies:           TBd

Facies Association:        Thrombolitic Boundstone

View of a dolomitised thrombolitic boundstone (TBd). This sample, a large algal lump, displaying
cryptalgal lamination (G7-A8) is probably a cross section through a domal thrombolite, although it
may also be a large algal clast. The sample also displays micro-peloids (I2, A3) and dolomite
microspar (F10, E4). Large, vuggy,  possibly  fenestral pores occur (G10, C2),  in addition to
intergranular (F5) and mouldic (F2) porosity.

                                         APPENDIX 3


                                                 Study Procedures



Standard procedures used by Robertson, Petroleum Division are given in this appendix.  Any
significant variations from these methods are indicated in the text.

CORE DESCRIPTION

Core description involves the detailed visual description of cores, normally side slabs. The various
parameters studied and recorded are discussed below.

i.      Lithology - all lithological terms used are standard.  For the limestone/dolomite transition,
        the following terms apply:
      0-10%         Limestone
      10%-50%      Dolomite limestone
      50%-90%       Calcareous dolomite
      90%-100%     Dolomite

      The Dunham (1962) scheme (wackestone, packstone, etc.) is used to classify depositional
         textures. Where the original texture is obscured by diagenetic effects the texture is recorded
        as ‘indeterminate’ (I).

ii.     Grain size - the modal grain size is plotted as a ‘moving line’; for bimodal grain size two
         lines are used. Thus in mud-supported limestones the matrix is recorded as mud-size, and the
       modal grain size of the grains is also recorded.

iii.    Sedimentary and diagenetic structures - physical, biological and diagenetic structures are
       recorded within the grain size column.

iv.    Grain types - are recorded within the lithology column, except where they are coarse enough
        to influence sedimentary structures. In this case they are also recorded with the structures.

v.     Fractures - in unoriented core, fracture dips are recorded in degrees relative to the core axis
          (i.e. 0° = vertical; 90° = horizontal).  Various fracture types are recorded, together with
        fracture cements.  Only natural fractures are recorded;  artificial fractures  (e.g. due to
        pressure release after coring - see Nelson, 1979) are disregarded. Where only side slabs of
        core are examined the presence of fractures may be seriously underestimated.

vi.    Colour - is visually assessed by comparison with the Geological Society of America ‘Rock-
       Color’ Chart.

                                   Page A3-1 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 104 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


vii.    Oil staining - presence of hydrocarbons is assessed by visual inspection.

viii.    Porosity and texture - cores are classified according to their Archie (1952)  I, II or III
         texture.  The  relative  distribution of  visible pore  size  in terms of Archie  classes B
       (<0.125 mm), C (0.125-2 mm) and D (>2 mm) is also recorded.

ix.      Stylolites - are recorded by their position, orientation, type, amplitude and mineral coating.

CORE TO LOG CORRELATION (DEPTH MATCHING)

Cores are depth matched against wireline logs using a combination of lithological and core analysis
data, and core gamma ray logs where available. Normally the gamma ray, neutron and density logs
are used in carbonate sequences. Core to log correlations were provided by Enterprise Oil for this
study.

THIN SECTION PREPARATION

Wherever possible, thin sections for petrographic analysis are prepared from the horizontal core
plugs, or their butt ends, so that measured porosity values can be directly related to the porosity
observed in thin section. Where core plugs are not available, thin sections are prepared from other
types of sample, e.g. core chips, sidewall cores, or ditch cuttings.

Samples are impregnated with a blue dyed fluorescing resin prior to thin section preparation. This
highlights porosity and also acts to hold together poorly consolidated and/or fractured samples.  In
the case of ditch cuttings a quantity of each is placed in a mould where they are embedded in resin.
Thin sections are made from the impregnated samples or, in the cases of ditch cuttings, moulds, using
standard procedures, finally being ground to a thickness of 30 µm.  Thin sections are then half-
stained with a mixture of alizarin red-S and potassium ferricyanide in acid solution (Dickson, 1965),
which enables the differentiation of various carbonate minerals, and then covered with a glass cover-
slip.

PETROGRAPHIC ANALYSIS

The half-stained thin sections are analysed using transmitted polarised light with normal petrographic
microscopes.  Data recorded include lithology, depositional texture (Dunham, 1962), grain size,
texture  (sorting, roundness) and assessment of  the percentages of  present and  original rock
components (e.g. grain types, mud, fossils, cements, pores, diagenetic minerals). Particular attention
is paid to the identification and distribution of pore types and cements.  Porosity nomenclature is
based on that of Choquette and Pray (1970) although the pore size scale used (see below) is based
upon a modified Wentworth scale.  Pore and grain sizes are estimated using an eyepiece graticule.
Percentages are estimated by use of comparison charts. Data for each thin section are recorded on a
petrographic data sheet and then input to a computer where they are available for statistical treatment
and graphic plotting.

[ صفحه 105 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
