                                         APPENDIX 4


                                 Glossary of Selected Geological Terms



Acicular - refers to the shape of crystals with a needle-like morphology.

Allochems - an inclusive term for carbonate grains or particles, as contrasted to carbonate mud
    matrix and clear calcite cement; includes fossils, ooids, pellets, etc. (Folk, 1959, 1962).

Anhedral  -  a  single  crystal  or  crystal  fabric  which  does  not show  well-defined  typical
     crystallographic forms.

Anhydrite - an evaporite mineral, CaSO4, found in sedimentary rocks.

Aragonite - an orthorhombic polymorph of calcite (CaCO3) which is metastable, is precipitated by
    many organisms within skeletal structures, and has greatest stability in seawater.

Argillite - a terrigenous sedimentary rock composed of fine grained silt to clay-size material that has
    been compacted and compressed.

Authigenic - a term implying development in place, usually after deposition.

Benthonic - an adjective referring to animals or plants that live on or in the sediments of the sea
     floor, including both mobile and sessile forms.

Bioclastic - grain of organic origin, e.g. shell or test.  Bioherm - a mound-like structure built by
    organisms in situ, such as a coral reef or rudist mound.

Biostrome - any sheet-like mass of purely organic material derived from sedentary organisms.

Blocky cement - mosaics of clearly equidimensional, or even granular, crystals rimming or filling
     voids; the crystals commonly lack parallel orientation; they tend to fill rather  than rim voids,
    and to be coarser and clearer than drusy crystals.

Borings - openings created in relatively rigid rock, shell, or other material by boring organisms. The
     rigid host substrate is the feature which distinguishes borings from burrows; the latter form in
    unconsolidated sediment.

Boudinage - a minor structure developed by the stretching of a competent bed along bedding planes,
     giving rise to pull-apart structures, tension cracks or necks, which may become filled with
    incompetent material from above or below.

                                   Page A4-1 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 108 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Boundstone - carbonate rock showing signs of grains being bound (by organisms) during deposition
    (Dunham, 1962).

Calcispheres - silt or sand sized spheres of clear (sparry) calcite, some with a discernible wall and
    some without. Probably of diverse origin, algal spores being a likely major variety.

Calcrete - an accumulation of predominantly fine grained, low magnesium calcite, having formed
     within the meteoric vadose zone by pedodiagenetic  alteration and/or replacement of any
     precursor host material.

Chalk - a limestone which predominantly consists of the remains of calcareous nannoplankton
     (especially  coccoliths)  and  microplankton  (especially  foraminifera).   Chalks  are  often
    considered to be soft and friable, although diagenetic alteration can lead to complete lithification
     of chalks.

Channel porosity - pore type with a marked elongation (lengths ten times or more than their cross-
     section diameter or width) in one or two dimensions relative to a third dimension and which are
     non-fabric selective. Normally the result of solution (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Clastic  -  as used by most sedimentary  petrologists, composed  of  particles  that have been
    mechanically transported, at least locally. Specifically includes limestones made up of fossils or
     other allochems that have been moved by waves or currents. (Note that most  facies mappers
    use clastic for terrigenous rocks and not limestones.)

Clay minerals - a group of minerals that are basically platy, layer lattice silicates, usually containing
    water and a complex of ions. They have variable properties, the most important being that some
     of them expand when they absorb water.

Clay size - a sediment, carbonate or terrigenous, that is composed of grains of less than 1/256 mm
     (3.9 µm).

Coated grains - a general term for grains with coatings or rims of calcium carbonate, includes ooids
    and superficial ooids, pisoids and algal-coated grains.

Dedolomitisation - a process whereby dolomite-bearing rock is converted to calcite-bearing rock.
    The precise diagenetic mechanism is not fully understood; however, percolating  waters are
    assumed to be responsible.

Detrital  - used in different ways by different authors and hence indefinable out of context.
    Sometimes synonymous with clastic, sometimes with terrigenous, and sometimes restricted to
    rocks composed of broken older rocks. Restricted to transported material.

Diagenesis - changes in sediments or sedimentary rocks which occur after deposition, but excluding
     processes involving high enough temperatures and pressures to be called metamorphism.

Disconformity - as used here refers to a minor type of unconformity representing an often short
     period of non-deposition of sediment. Such breaks can be recognised by such physical features
     as burrowed or bored surfaces, washouts or intraformational conglomerates (see hiatus).

                                   Page A4-2 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 109 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Dolomite - CaMg (CO3)2 true dolomite contains equal numbers of Mg and Ca ions in an ordered
     structure but many dolomites do not contain the ordered structure. The term is generally used to
     refer to any rock with more than 15% MgCO3 or to a mineral with more than 28% MgCO3.

Dolomite, saddle - a variety of dolomite with a warped crystal lattice, ranging from rhombohedra
    with slight facial curvature to classic saddle forms.  It is characterised by curved crystal faces
    and cleavages, undulose extinction, pearly  lustre, and an abundance   of very small fluid
     inclusions (Radke and Mathis, 1980).

Dolospar - clear crystals of dolomite (see Spar).

Drusy cement - mosaics of clearly elongate, or even acicular, crystals rimming or filling voids; the
     crystals are commonly oriented so that their length is roughly perpendicular to the walls of the
     void; they tend to rim rather than fill their void.

Enterolithic anhydrite - anhydrite exhibiting irregularly folded and contorted layers formed during
     the displacive growth of anhydrite.

Euhedral  -  refers  to  a  single  crystal  or  crystal  fabric which shows  well  defined,  typical
     crystallographic forms.

Facies - the sum total of features which characterise a sediment as having been deposited in a given
    environment.

Faunal assemblage - is defined here as the group of organisms which characterise a given rock
     interval.

Fenestrae - (fenestral fabric) primary or penecontemporaneous gaps in rock framework larger than
     grain-supported  interstices.  Commonly  associated with  algal mats and can  result from
     shrinkage, gas formation, organic decay, etc.

Fibrous - with reference to sparry calcite cement it is defined as including crystals with length-to-
    width ratios greater than 6:1.

Firmground  - a partially cemented or compacted surface at the sediment-water interface (see
    Hardground). Common in chalky sediments.

Fracture porosity - porosity formed by fracturing.  Grades into breccia porosity with increasing
     dislocation or ‘chaos’ (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Framework porosity  - primary porosity created by the in-place growth of a carbonate rock
    framework. Equivalent to the ‘growth-framework’ porosity of Choquette and Pray (1970).

Geopetal structure - a sedimentary structure(s) which enables the determination of the original
     orientation of the rock strata.

Grains  -  1.  solid  particles whose  physical  limits may encompass many  crystalline  entities.
     Distinctions between coarse grained and coarsely crystalline are not always observed, but  are
    fundamental; 2. the friable aggregates of silt-sized carbonate crystals that are forming today on
     the Bahaman Platform from the partial cementation of crystals in contact with each other. The
     particles are coarser than 0.02 mm (20 µm).

                                   Page A4-3 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 110 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id



Grain debris - used here for silt-sized (20-63 µm) grains of indeterminate origin.

Grain-supported - refers to the fabric of a rock in which the grains (allochems) are in contact with
    each other, even though they may have a mud (micrite) matrix.

Grainstone - a grain supported carbonate rock in which no carbonate mud is present (Dunham,
    1962).

Gypsum - an evaporite mineral, CaSO4.2H2O, found in sedimentary rocks.

Hardground - a cemented surface at the sediment-water interface during deposition of a sedimentary
    sequence.  It is usually marked by borings, marine cements, eroded lithified clasts and possibly
    by accumulation of phosphatic or iron materials, and it represents a period of non-deposition (a
     Hiatus).

Hiatus - a time gap, marked by a period of non-deposition.

High magnesium calcite - calcite containing greater than 4% magnesium ions in the lattice.  It is
    found in some organic skeletons and as a cement in marine and marginal marine environments.
       It is not very stable when subjected to pore waters with a low Mg/Ca ratio, but loses its Mg by
     ionic substitution and with little change in morphology.

Hypidiotopic texture - descriptive term for fabrics of diagenetically altered carbonate rocks in
    which the majority of the constituent crystals are subhedral (i.e. have partially developed crystal
     faces).

Idiotopic texture - descriptive term for fabrics of diagenetically altered carbonate rocks in which the
     majority of the constituent crystals are euhedral (i.e. have well developed crystal facies).

Intercrystalline porosity - porosity between individual crystals of somewhat equant and equal size
     (as in many porous dolomites) (Choquette and Pray, 1970).  Separated into finer than and
     coarser than 20 microns.

Intergranular porosity - porosity between grains (grains are particles coarser than 20 microns)
    (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Interstices - technically, voids; but used for areas that were voids in the initial sediment, though they
     are now filled.

Intertidal zone - zone located between high tide and low tide and influenced by periodic subaerial
    exposure.    Significant  features  of  these  mudflats  are  thin,  flat  sand  lenses and  thin
     interlamination of mud and sand,  flaser and  lenticular bedding and moderate  to strong
     bioturbation.

Intraclast  - a fragment  of penecontemporaneous, commonly weakly  consolidated, carbonate
    sediment that has been eroded from the sea bottom and redeposited nearby.

Intragranular porosity - porosity within individual grains (Choquette and Pray, 1970).  Restricted
     to pores coarser than 20 microns; finer pores are matrix pores.

                                   Page A4-4 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 111 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Inversion (polymorphic transformation) - the change of aragonite to low Mg calcite without the
     creation of a void by the migration of a fluid front through the crystal (Folk, 1965).

Lime mud - calcite, aragonite or high Mg calcite finer than 20 microns (synonymous with micrite).

Limpid - clear, transparent (referring to crystals).

Lithoclast - a grain that was lithified before it was eroded.  It may be identified by shape and by
     truncated grains at the edge of the clast.

Lithofacies - facies which are particularly characterised by rock type.   It should be noted that a
     particular facies may be diachronous, and this may only be detected if the fossil  evidence is
     adequate.

Low magnesium calcite - calcite with less than 4% Mg ions in the lattice. The most stable and
   common form of calcite in the fossil record.

Matrix porosity - loosely this is the porosity within the matrix of a carbonate sediment or rock
    (Choquette and Pray, 1970). Used here for all non-intercrystalline pores finer than 20 microns.

Megapore - a pore diameter size class with a range of 4 mm to 256 mm (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Meniscus - refers to a carbonate cement type formed during vadose diagenesis where cement crystals
    form only at or near grain contacts in the positions a water meniscus would occupy.

Mesapore - a pore diameter size class with a range of 0.625 mm to 4 mm (Choquette and Pray,
    1970).

Meteoric water - a term applied to freshwater.

Micrite - microcrystalline calcite; used both as a synonym for carbonate mud (or ‘ooze’) and for rock
    composed of carbonate mud (calcilutite) (Folk, 1959, 1962).

Micritisation  - the conversion of carbonate crystals (particularly within skeletal fragments) to
     crystals of micrite.   It may involve a change in mineralogy, particularly with degradation by
    boring algae and fungi and by bacterial decomposition.

Microfabric - the features of structure and texture of a rock seen on a microscopic scale during
    scanning electron microscopy.

Micropore - a pore diameter size class ranging up to 0.625 mm (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Microspar - generally 5 to 15 micron sized calcite produced by recrystallisation of micrite; can be as
     coarse as 30 microns (Folk, 1965).

Microstalactitic (pendulous or gravitational cement) - a descriptive term for cements which are
     concentrated on the bottom sides of grains. Such textures generally form in vadose or  upper
     intertidal areas where pores are only partially water-filled and in which water droplets can hang
    from the undersides of grains.

Microstylolite - a pressure solution surface of less than 1 mm in amplitude.

                                   Page A4-5 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 112 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id



Mould; mouldic porosity - a pore formed by selective removal, normally by dissolution, of a former
     individual constituent of the sediment of rock such as a shell or ooid.  Often used  with
    modifying prefixes: biomouldic, etc. (Choquette and Pray, 1970).

Mud - particles finer than 0.02 mm (20 µm).

Mudstone - a carbonate rock composed of carbonate mud with less than 10% allochems (Dunham,
    1962).

Neomorphic recrystallisation - process whereby original crystal units of a particular size and
    morphology become converted into crystal units with different (generally coarser) grain size or
    morphology, but with the mineral species remaining constant.

Neomorphic spar - spar that results from processes of neomorphism, usually by recrystallisation
     (Bathurst, 1975).

Neomorphism - a collective term for inversion and recrystallisation processes.

Nodule - a local concentration of chemical compounds in sediments or soils, which has formed
     within the sediment or as a result of sediment-forming processes. They are mostly rounded, but
    vary from perfectly spherical to a variety of irregular shapes (Fairbridge and Bourgeois, 1978).

Oncoid (oncolith) - a coated grain of algal origin: a spheroidal form of algal stromatolite showing a
     series of concentric (often irregular) laminations. These unattached stromatolites are produced
    by mechanical turning or rolling, exposing new surfaces to algal growth.

Oolite (ooids) - Oolite is a rock composed of ooids. Ooids are spherical to ellipsoidal bodies, 0.25 to
    2.00 mm in diameter with a nucleus and radial or concentric structure  (calcareous, haematitic,
     siliceous, etc.) generally grown in an agitated environment.

Ooze - carbonate mud; either the original soft sediment or its consolidated equivalent.

Orthochemical - a rock constituent that is a normal chemical precipitate, as contrasted to fossils,
     ooids, or other mechanically or biologically deposited constituents (Folk, 1959).

Overgrowth cementation  - cementation by overgrowth on existing crystals; normally used to
     indicate overgrowths on micrite grains as seen under the scanning electron microscope.

Packstone - a grain supported carbonate rock containing a carbonate mud matrix (Dunham, 1962).

Pelagic - mode of life of those animals which live in the open sea, but not on the sea floor, including
    free-swimming (nektonic) forms and forms which  float passively in the surface   waters
     (planktonic).

Pellet - a grain composed of micrite, generally lacking significant internal structure and often ovoid
     in shape. Many, but not all, pellets are of faecal origin. Most pellets in limestones are very fine
    sand to coarse silt size.

                                   Page A4-6 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 113 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Peloid - an allochem formed of cryptocrystalline or microcrystalline carbonate, irrespective of size or
     origin. This term allows reference to grains composed of micrite or microspar without the need
     to imply any particular mode of origin (can thus include pellets, some vague intraclasts,
     micritized fossils, ooids, etc.).

Penecontemporaneous - generally referring to cements or replacement textures indicating that, in
     the opinion of the user, the feature or mineral formed at almost the same time as the original
    sediment was deposited, that is, close to the sediment-air or sediment-water interface.

Phreatic - the zone of water saturation below the water table. There are marine, meteoric or mixed
     phreatic zones.

Pisoid - oolitically coated, often compound grain, greater than 2 mm in diameter.

Planktonic - a term used to describe the mode of life of those animals which live in the open sea and
     float passively in the surface waters.

Poikilotopic - description of a diagenetic fabric within a sedimentary rock in which larger crystals
    enclose smaller crystals or detrital grains.

Pressure solution - the dissolution of carbonate crystals by the effects of stress. The stress may be
     of tectonic origin or, more commonly, as a result of the overburden.  Carbonate minerals are
     dissolved along points of contact, forming pressure solution surfaces, and the dissolved material
   may  migrate  to  areas  of low  stress where  precipitation  (as cement)  may occur  (cf.
     Microstylolite, Solution seam, Stylolite).

Primary porosity - porosity present in the rock or sediment immediately after final deposition.

Pseudomorph - one mineral occurring in the crystal form of another.

Pseudospar - a neomorphic (recrystallisation) calcite fabric, with average crystal size larger than 30
    microns (Folk, 1965).

Recrystallisation - the change of crystals in one mineral species to crystals of a different size or
    shape  (usually  larger  =  aggrading)  of  the  same  mineral  species   (cf.  neomorphic
     recrystallisation).

Reef - a mass of organic skeletal material consisting in part of organisms which have grown in-place,
     in part of material derived from organic debris which has been transported to  the site of the
     reef, and in part of chemically precipitated material.

Replacement - the change of one mineral to a mineral with different composition (e.g. calcite may
    be replaced by dolomite, or anhydrite).

Sabkha  - generally characterised by very low surface slope (<1/2000) occurring inland of the
     coastline. The ground-water table lies 1m-2m below the surface. Continued evaporation from
     the surface draws water upward through the capillary zone above the water table, and near-
     surface salt minerals are also precipitated by this mechanism.

Sea floor - the sediment-water interface below the sea, but also including sediments just below this
     interface that may be disturbed and redeposited.

                                   Page A4-7 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 114 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id



Secondary porosity - any porosity created in a sediment after final deposition.

Shelter porosity  - a type of primary interparticle porosity created by the sheltering effect of
     relatively large sedimentary particles which prevent the infilling of pore space beneath them by
     finer clastic particles.

Skeletal - pertaining to debris derived from organisms that secrete hard material around or within
     organic tissue. The term ‘bioclastic’ is considered to be synonymous with ‘skeletal’.

Solution seam - a layer bounded by undulose surfaces, containing a high proportion of relatively
     insoluble material, concentrated as a result of non-sutured seam pressure  solution; primarily
     within carbonate lithologies.

Spar (sparry calcite) - calcite sufficiently coarsely crystalline to appear fairly transparent in thin
     section, as contrasted to dark, cloudy-appearing carbonate mud or micrite (Folk, 1959, 1962).

Spastolith - compacted, deformed ooid showing compression taken up by lateral shearing of the
     outer cortex, characterised by structures such as notched and stretched grains, grains connected
    by narrow necks and several grains linked in long zig-zag chains (Conley, 1977; Scholle, 1978).

Stromatolites - structures of sediment trapped by filamentous algae and subsequently cemented.

Structure grumeleuse - a clotted structure in micrite, resembling poorly formed pellets which may
    merge into each other (Bathurst, 1975, p. 512).

Stylolite - a jagged, columnar surface in carbonate rocks which may be at any orientation relative to
    bedding; often associated with large amounts of insoluble material, and produced by pressure
     solution and grain interpenetration.

Subaerial - terrestrial environment beyond the influence of the marine environment, exposed to
    weathering and soil-forming processes.

Subtidal zone - zone located below low tide, composed of shallow marine sediments.  Primary
    sedimentary structures are almost completely destroyed by bioturbation.

Sucrose  - carbonate rocks  that have appreciable  intercrystal pore space and are dominantly
    composed of somewhat equant, uniformly sized, euhedral to subhedral crystals.

Supratidal zone  - zone located above high tide and only periodically influenced by the sea.
     Characterised by clayey sand and clayey silt layers with a nodular appearance in a moderate
    environment and by chaotic layers due to crystal growth, desiccation cracks, mud  flakes and
   mud pebbles in an arid climate. Stromatolites and fenestrae may also be present.

Syneresis - spontaneous liberation of a liquid and concomitant shrinkage of a gel (clays) during
     ageing.

Synsedimentary - processes occurring during sedimentation.

Syntaxial - refers to overgrowths which are in optical continuity with their underlying grains.

                                   Page A4-8 r


────────────────────────────────────────────
[ صفحه 115 از 133 ]
────────────────────────────────────────────
Enterprise Oil plc                                                         Report No. 8525/Id


Terrigenous - derived from the land area and transported mechanically to the basin of deposition;
    commonly ‘non-carbonate’ (e.g. terrigenous sand vs. carbonate sand).

Unconformity - the concept is a complex one and needs to be considered in terms of three major
     aspects: time, deposition and structure. Four types of unconformity have been recognised:

     Parallel unconformity - the lower and upper series of beds dip the same amount and in the
        same direction.
    Angular unconformity - there is a discrepancy in the angle of dip between the older and
        younger strata.
    Non-depositional unconformity - representing a period of non-deposition and recognised by
         the absence of faunal zones or by presence of hardgrounds, exposure surfaces, etc.
     Heterolithic unconformity - where sediment is deposited on metamorphic or igneous rocks or
        on an ancient land surface.

Vadose - designates the area above the water table (zone of aeration) in which both water and air are
     present in pores.

Vug, vugular porosity - a pore that is somewhat equant, is larger than 1/16 mm in diameter, and
    does not specifically conform in position, shape or boundary to particular fabric elements of the
     host rock (e.g. is not fabric selective).  Generally formed by solution (Choquette and Pray,
    1970).

Wackestone - mud supported carbonate rock with over 10% allochems (Dunham, 1962).

Xenotopic - descriptive term relating to the fabric of a diagenetically altered carbonate rock in which
     the majority of the constituent crystals are anhedral (i.e. crystal faces absent).
